Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

Medabots Classics Plus coming to PC in December in Japan

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Publisher Imagineer and developer SUCCESS Corporation will release Medabots Classics Plus for PC via Steam in both Kabuto Ver. and Kuwagata Ver. in December in Japan for 5,478 yen each, the companies announced.

Medabots Classics Plus first launched for Switch on November 12, 2020 in Japan.

Here is an overview of the collection, via its store pages:

A historic collection of eight games from Medabots series.

This collection includes the five numbered games in the series, from Medabots to Medabots 5: Susutake Mura no Tenkousei, as well as Medabots Navi, Medabots G, and Medabots Two: CORE. Players can enjoy a diverse range of genres, spanning RPG, strategy RPG, and action.

While the original appeal of the games remain unchanged, the collection introduces convenient features like the ability to save and load at any time, a Robattle skip feature, and a high-speed mode. It also supports online battles and parts trading between both the Kabuto Ver. and Kuwagata Ver.

This is the definitive Medabots series collection.

Amy Poehler And Steve Carell Bonded Over How Poorly The Office And Parks And Rec Went Their First Seasons

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Thank Goodness A Nobody Wants This Star And I Have The Same Weird Emotions About Morgan And Sasha

I Loved That St. Denis Medical Straight Up Referenced The Pitt, And I Had To Ask The Creator Why

I Started Watching Big Brother Because Of Survivor, But Producers Were Shook By All That Fan Hate

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If Parks and Recreation and The Office have one thing in common, it's that both series took a little while to find their footing. Of course, these two comedies have more than one thing in common -- both aired on NBC around the same time, both were created (or co-created in Parks & Rec's case, alongside Michael Schur) by Greg Daniels, and both debuted with big expectations from fans of an existing series. It's no surprise that Amy Poehler would find some very common ground with Steve Carell when talking about their experiences on their respective shows.

Not all of the best sitcoms of all time got off to an amazing start. The Office and Parks and Recreation are two perfect examples of shows that needed more than a few episodes to find their groove. In March, back when Steve Carell's new HBO series Rooster was just getting started, the Office star sat down with Amy Poehler on her Good Hang podcast. Among the topics that came up was how poorly their earlier sitcoms were initially received by viewers.

As Poehler tells it, there are two people that she's been told she's a "poor man's version of," and Carell is one of them. She went on to laugh about how terribly the debut of Parks and Recreation went, saying:

Parks and Rec had the worst launch ever. Everyone was like, 'This is not The Office, we don't like this.' I just remembered we're like -- 'You're not Steve and you're not -- we don't like it!' And I think I just dissociated and was like, 'well.'"

That viewers might have been looking for more of what they'd come to know and love from The Office -- and Carell's character, Michael Scott -- is not that surprising, given its creators and the fact that it was another single-camera mockumentary-style workplace comedy. In the case of Parks and Rec, the focus was on the office workers at the Parks and Recreation department of a small town in Indiana. That included Poehler's optimistic and determined Leslie Knope.

When Greg Daniels and Michael Schur's new series debuted in spring of 2009, it was around the same time that The Office was wrapping up its fifth season. Months prior to Parks and Rec's debut, The Office had its post-Super Bowl double episode, "Stress Relief," which was watched by millions (and is remembered as one of the best episodes of the series). The Office was firmly established with its fanbase, while Parks and Rec was just getting warmed up.

It's almost easy to forget that The Office had faced a similar challenge years earlier, as Carell noted in his conversation with Poehler:

Our pilot was the lowest casting pilot in the history, I think, of NBC. People really hated it ... They actively hated this show, and I don't quite know how it got legs after that.

Lest we not forget, The Office didn't debut to universal praise and sky-high ratings when it arrived in 2005, and it was nearly cancelled after its first season. The show arrived on the heels of the success of the 2001 Ricky Gervais sitcom that started it all, and to say that the U.S. version of the beloved UK comedy had big shoes to fill would be an understatement.

Personally, I didn't hate the first season of the U.S. version of The Office when it aired, but I also wouldn't argue with many of the criticisms of the show's first six episodes. That's a whole other conversation though. Getting back to the topic at hand, in his conversation with Amy Poehler, Carell called himself out as "a poor man's Ricky Gervais."

I don't think anyone would say that about Carell today, but he certainly had a high bar to meet when leading the U.S. version of The Office. And from what he said, he intentionally didn't watch more than a minute of Gervais in Office:

I watched like a minute of him, and he was so good, and so specific, and so funny, I thought, if I watch a second more, I'm just going to go on an audition with that. I won't be able to even imagine it a different way.

There really is only one David Brent, so it's understandable that Carell wouldn't want to try to recreate that character in Michael Scott. Ultimately, Carell's character became iconic in his own right, thanks in large part to the actor's comedic brilliance. Several seasons after he made his debut as Michael Scott, Carell had a say in how his character would exit the series.

All of this to say, I can see why Carell and Poehler would connect on this particular topic, as both of them saw success in their respective roles and shows, but it wasn't instant and both had to find their way out from under the shadow of other performances. Each show had to find their own way to success, and it's just really fortunate that both were given time and seasons to do so. I can't imagine a TV world without Michael Scott or Leslie Knope!

You can watch Parks and Recreation and The Office streaming with a Peacock subscription.

She graduated from SUNY Cortland with BA in Communication Studies and a minor in Cinema Studies. When she isn't working, she's probably thinking about work, or reading (or listening to a book), and making sure her cats are living their absolute best feline lives.

If I Could Buy Just 1 Stock in November and Hold for 5 Years, It Would Be Alphabet

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alphabet is already one of the world's most powerful technology companies. With AI creating another major growth opportunity, this could be a stock worth holding through 2031.

If I could buy just one stock this month and hold it for the long term, Alphabet (GOOGL +1.18%) (GOOG +0.73%) would be at the top of my list.

The reason is simple: I believe artificial intelligence (AI) could become one of the biggest investment opportunities of the next decade, and Alphabet is positioned to benefit from it in several ways.

But unlike some companies that depend almost entirely on AI spending, Alphabet already owns highly profitable businesses that generate billions of dollars in revenue.

That combination makes it an attractive long-term candidate.

Alphabet is much more than Google Search. It owns YouTube, Google Cloud, Android, and a range of widely used digital services. These businesses give it access to billions of users and provide multiple ways to monetize AI.

That Alphabet has multiple ways to win in the years to come is an important advantage.

Unlike most AI investments, Alphabet's investment case isn't based entirely on future promises. Even without including future opportunities, Alphabet is already a massive, profitable business.

For perspective, in the third quarter of 2026, revenue increased 25% year over year to $127.6 billion. Operating income rose 30% to $40.6 billion.

Google Cloud was particularly impressive, with revenue more than doubling to $30.7 billion. Its operating income also increased to $11.1 billion from $3.6 billion a year earlier. Meanwhile, Google Search revenue grew 12%.

These numbers demonstrate something important: Alphabet's AI opportunity is already translating into meaningful business growth, particularly in cloud computing.

Better still, its existing profitable businesses also provide a foundation to fund further investment in AI. That diversification matters over five years. If one part of the AI market disappoints, Alphabet still has other businesses capable of generating revenue and profits.

There is, however, a reason investors should not overlook the risks. Building AI infrastructure is expensive. Alphabet is investing heavily in data centers, servers, networking equipment, and other computing resources. In Q3 alone,capital expenditure (capex) reached $51.4 billion. That was enough to push quarterly free cash flow into negative territory, at $9.1 billion.

Management expects full-year 2026 capex of $195 billion to $205 billion, with spending increasing significantly again in 2027.

This is a substantial commitment. If AI demand continues growing and these investments generate attractive returns, the spending could strengthen Alphabet's competitive position for years. But if demand disappoints or computing capacity becomes less valuable than expected, the company could end up with expensive infrastructure that takes longer to pay off.

There is also a competitive risk. Companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic are challenging Google's position in AI. Alphabet has the resources to compete, but it is not guaranteed to win every battle.

For me, the attraction to Alphabet is the combination of an established business and a significant new growth opportunity. Alphabet already has products used by billions of people. It has a profitable advertising business, a rapidly growing cloud division, proprietary AI models, and the infrastructure to integrate these technologies.

Over the next five years, I would watch three things closely: whether Search maintains its competitive position, whether Google Cloud continues growing profitably, and whether AI investments translate into stronger free cash flow.

If it delivers on all three areas, the stock is well positioned to create enormous shareholder value in the next five years.

Lawrence Nga has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Mark Zuckerberg Meta AI Predicts Top 3 ZK Cryptos

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Mark Zuckerberg’s Meta AI predicts the top 3 ZK-based cryptocurrencies for 2027. Zero-knowledge technology is moving from an experimental scaling tool to core infrastructure for privacy, institutional finance, and high-throughput applications.

Heading into 2027, three projects stand out for a combination of technical progress, real-world traction, and narrative strength: zkSync (ZK), Starknet (STRK), and Zcash (ZEC).

With all three projects at one point in time being mentioned by Ethereum’s creator, Vitalik Buterin, they are all viable picks for the top ZK-based tokens, but stick around until the end to find out which one Meta AI predicts will have a huge 2027.

zkSync remains one of the strongest pure-play ZK rollups on Ethereum, with Matter Labs pushing hard into institutional territory. Its Prividium platform is designed for regulated entities, enabling private, permissioned environments while retaining Ethereum settlement.

Recent progress includes SOC 2 Type 2 certification, the launch of Prividium Commander for institutional control planes, and partnerships aimed at Japanese financial institutions and U.S. regional banks exploring tokenized deposits.

The Airbender Prime prover achieved a notable milestone by proving the vast majority of Ethereum blocks in under 10 seconds on a single high-end GPU, signaling real-time proving capability.

Technically, ZK has been consolidating at low absolute levels after a prolonged downtrend, typical of many L2 tokens in a risk-off environment. A sustained break above recent range highs on rising volume would signal renewed accumulation.

The institutional narrative is its clearest differentiator; if banks and enterprises continue adopting Prividium-style infrastructure, demand for the token and network activity could expand significantly in 2027.

Starknet leads in STARK-based technology, offering transparent, quantum-resistant proofs and the Cairo programming environment. It continues to innovate with features such as Shadow Accounts for improved privacy in DeFi interactions.

The network has explored paths toward greater sovereignty, including discussions around becoming an independent L1 while targeting stronger quantum resistance.

Its developer ecosystem remains one of the deepest in the ZK space, based on historical repository and contributor metrics.STRK currently trades near multi-month lows around the $0.038–$0.040 area after a sharp correction from higher levels earlier in the year.

The chart shows a classic post-hype compression. A decisive reclaim of the $0.05–$0.06 zone with expanding volume would be an early bullish signal.

Meta AI predicts that Starknet’s combination of technical sophistication and potential architectural evolution positions it well if the market rewards high-performance validity rollups and privacy enhancements in 2027.

$ZEC Has been consolidating for the past 6 weeks.The structure itself doesn't look bad considering the market environment we've been in.But you'd want to see a convincing break and hold above that ~$1375 level first. That should kick off the next bigger move.On the… pic.twitter.com/YVkFG5fRAq

As the original major ZK-SNARK privacy coin, Zcash benefits from a clear, focused use case and ongoing protocol upgrades. The NU7 upgrade improved shielded transaction performance and advanced its privacy pool architecture.

Unlike many L2 tokens, Zcash offers native, opt-in (and increasingly default-friendly) transaction privacy with a mature shielded pool system. It maintains relatively strong liquidity and institutional accessibility compared with smaller privacy coins.

ZEC has shown relative resilience versus many altcoins, supported by its privacy narrative amid growing regulatory and surveillance concerns.

Technically, it has established higher relative support levels than pure L2 tokens. Continued migration into shielded pools and any further usability improvements could drive renewed interest.

Meta AI predicts that zkSync edges ahead for institutional product-market fit, Starknet for pure technical ambition and STARK leadership, and Zcash for proven privacy utility.

All three benefit from the broader maturation of zero-knowledge proofs. If Ethereum scaling demand rebounds and privacy becomes a stronger market theme in 2027, these projects are well-placed to outperform within the ZK sector.

Mesa Labs (MLAB) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Debt paydown and a new buyback shape a quarter with mixed segment results.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Mesa Laboratories, Inc. (MLAB -0.34%) reported its second quarter fiscal 2027 financial results while providing its first full-year guidance for the year ending March 31, 2027. Management initiated a strategic realignment of its four operating segments into two distinct groups: quality control businesses and life sciences tools businesses. The company implemented a restructuring plan for the Clinical Genomics division to focus on profitability and high-utilization accounts rather than revenue scale. Management reported progress in addressing manufacturing delays in the Sterilization and Disinfection Control segment and announced a $40 million share repurchase authorization to provide capital allocation flexibility while continuing to prioritize debt reduction.

Whatever business we own, our job is to run it as well as it can be run. To be good stewards of it. A business that consumes cash leaves very few choices about how to invest in it. The business that earns its keep and generates cash. Gives us many more options. In clinical genomics, we are building a focused profitable business that we expect to generate cash.

That is the right way to run any business we own and it is what puts us in a position to make good long term decisions. The restructure was not a decision to exit any specific end market. If a customer use the instrument at scale, we want to serve them wherever they are. It was a difficult decision for the people affected which is why we have approached that with due care. John V. Sakys: Thank you, Siddhartha, and good afternoon, everyone. I will stay in the numbers for today's call. I will first discuss the income statement, the restructuring, statement of cash flows, and the balance sheet. And then the mechanics of our guidance. Unless I note otherwise, comparisons are to the second quarter of fiscal year 26. Total revenues were $62.3 million, million up 2.6%. With no acquisitions in the past 12 months, organic growth was also 2.6%, and constant currency organic growth which excludes a currency headwind of about 50 basis points was 3.1%. And higher revenues on a partially fixed cost base. BPD's gross profit percentage increased 76 basis points to 65.1%. Primarily due to favorable product mix as higher margin immunoassays revenues and consumables representing a greater proportion of the division's revenues. And lower margins in the prior year from the impact of tariffs and foreign currency. Calibration Solutions gross profit percentage declined a 110 basis points to 59.6%.

Primarily due to unfavorable product mix and higher transportation costs, and clinical genomics declined 80 basis points to 53.9%. Primarily due to unfavorable product mix as higher margin consumables represented a lower portion of revenues as well as lower revenues on a partially fixed cost base. Operating expenses declined 3.4% to 31.5 million primarily due to lower stock based compensation as the prior year included costs associated with multiyear equity awards granted to our former CEO, lower expense related to estimated uncollectible accounts receivable, and lower amortization as certain intangible assets became fully amortized. GAAP operating income increased 77.6% to $8.4 million. Net income was $4.3 million or $0.75 per diluted share compared with $0.45 a year ago.

Adjusted EBITDA increased 21.1% to $17.3 million or 27.8% of revenues compared with 23.5% a year ago. iHeartMedia (IHRT) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Podcast growth offset broadcast weakness as management updated its outlook.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

iHeartMedia, Inc. (IHRT +0.00%) reported consolidated revenue of $1,030 million for the third quarter, a 3.3% increase compared to the prior year period. Management stated that growth was driven by the Digital Audio Group and Audio & Media Services Group, which offset a revenue decline in the Multiplatform Group. The company indicated that its strategic shift toward programmatic advertising and the rollout of the AudioGraph platform are intended to address current headwinds in broadcast radio monetization. Management also announced plans to restructure its financial reporting in 2027 by establishing podcasting as a standalone segment to provide transparency into that growth engine. The company emphasized its focus on optimizing its capital structure and achieving further cost efficiencies through the deployment of artificial intelligence and other technological solutions.

Operator: Hello, everyone. Thank you for joining us and welcome to the iHeartMedia third quarter 2026 earnings call. After today's prepared remarks, we will host a question-and-answer session. If you would like to ask a question, please press *1 to raise your hand. To withdraw your question, press *1 again. I will now hand the conference over to Andrey Hart, senior vice president of investor relations. Andrey, please go ahead.

Andrey Hart: Good afternoon, everyone. Thank you for taking the time to join us for our third quarter 2026 earnings call. Joining me for today's discussion are Bob Pittman, our Chairman and CEO; Richard J. Bressler, our President and COO; and Michael McGuinness, our CFO. At the conclusion of our prepared remarks, management will take your questions. In addition to our press release, we have an earnings presentation available on our website that you can use to follow along with our remarks. Please note that this call may include forward looking statements regarding our financial performance and operating results.

These statements are based on management's current expectations, and actual results could differ from what is stated as a result of certain factors identified on today's call and in the company's SEC filings. Including our recent 8-K filing. Additionally, during this call, we will refer to certain non GAAP financial measures, Reconciliations between GAAP and non GAAP financial measures are included in our earnings release earnings presentation and our SEC filings. Which are available on the Investor Relations section of our website. And now I will turn the call over to Bob.

Robert W. Pittman: Thanks, Andrey, and good afternoon, everyone. In the third quarter, our consolidated revenue was $1.03 billion. Up 3.3% compared to the prior year quarter and below our guidance range of up mid single digits, driven by softness in our Multiplatform Group segment. Excluding the impact of political, our consolidated revenue was up 0.9%. We generated adjusted EBITDA of 170 million in the third quarter, below our previously provided guidance range of $180 million to $220 million Our digital audio group led by podcasting and our audio and media services group both continued to deliver strong results and profitability.

However, our multiplatform group, which importantly continues to have real strength with the consumer base, ongoing industry and macroeconomic revenue headwinds that impacted financial results more than anticipated. But we believe many of these multiplatform group revenue challenges are addressable. And that the strategic monetization initiatives we have put in place position us to return the Multiplatform Group back to earnings growth. The shortfall in Multiplatform Group performance was driven primarily by 4 factors. First, lower than anticipated political advertising revenue for radio. Second, a number of advertisers, especially larger advertisers, delayed their advertising spend until after the election. Third, audio advertising buying is moving faster to programmatic than we had anticipated.

In addition to the targeting, measurability, and attribution that have historically separated broadcast radio from digital media, This is ahead of our complete rollout of our own radio programmatic and attribution offerings. Fourth, a much more challenging macroeconomic environment than we had hoped for and 1 which we expect will continue through the remainder of the year. I will discuss each of these factors in greater detail, I will also outline how we plan to manage our business starting in 2027, when we expect to present podcasting as a separate reporting segment. Turning to our individual operating segments.

The Digital Audio Group generated third quarter revenue of $389 million up 13.8% versus prior year and within the range of our previous provided guidance of up low teens. The Digital Audio Group generated third quarter adjusted EBITDA of $127 million down 2.3% versus prior year as it was burdened by high higher noncash marketing expenses. The adjusted EBITDA margin was 32.8%. Within the digital audio group, our podcasting revenue strong momentum continues, with $174 million for the quarter, up 24.6% compared to prior year of $140 million above our guidance of up approximately 20%. We continue to be the number 1 podcast publisher in American audience as reported by both Podtrac and Triton.

The 2 largest measurement services that measure actual downloads and users. Podcasting continues to be the strongest growth component for the company, and the overall podcast industry growth remains strong as well. Our expansion in the video podcasting represents an entirely new market for our podcasting product creating additional revenue opportunities. Beyond our video podcast content is now distributed across major platforms including Netflix, Apple, Amazon, Disney Plus, and Hulu, as well as our own iHeartRadio app. Strongly positioning us to participate in this new and important revenue stream. As video and audio podcast consumption continues to grow, we believe we are well positioned to take advantage of both.

In the third quarter, digital ex-podcast revenue grew 6.3% compared to prior year within our previously provided guidance of up mid single digits, and it continues to be an important growth vector for the company. Turning now to the multiplatform group, which includes our broadcast radio networks and events businesses. Third quarter revenue was $562 million down 4.9% versus prior year and below our guidance range of approximately flat. Excluding the impact of political advertising, multiplatform group revenue was down 6.6%. The Multiplatform Group's adjusted EBITDA was $68 million compared to $119 million in the prior year.

Noncash marketing expense related to co marketing partnerships supporting our Audiograph platform which we will discuss in more detail, drove the majority of the negative impact on our multiplatform group's adjusted EBITDA in this quarter. Now let me expand on 4 drivers that negatively impacted the multiplatform group's performance in Q3. First, we generated less political revenue than we expected. Accounting for about a third of the underperformance in Q3 revenue. As we mentioned on our last earnings call, we expected that this was going to be a robust political advertising year for broadcast radio in addition to media overall. And we anticipated even doing better than the 2024 presidential year.

But we actually came in below the 2022 non-presidential election year on multiplatform group revenue because of 2 major factors. In response to strong political advertising demand, local TV broadcasters created more inventory and therefore held on to a lot of the political advertising dollars that historically would spill over to broadcast radio when TV reached sell out. In fact, according to Ad Impact, approximately 50% of all political spending this cycle was on broadcast television, which is much larger than its typical 15% share overall advertising spend. And the other factor was that even in audio advertising, a greater percentage of political advertising spend moved to programmatic platforms in this cycle than we had anticipated.

While this is in line with the broader transition to programmatic, it happened to of political inventory faster than we thought it would. it is important to note that overall in this political cycle, radio share of political advertising is actually lower than radio share of the overall advertising marketplace. Exactly the opposite experience of local broadcast TV. While disappointing, both developments reinforce our belief that expanding our broadcast radio programmatic capabilities through our Audiograph platform is the right strategic plan.

Second, some brands specifically larger brands, delayed deploying their budgets until after the election for 2 reasons: additional clutter combined with the higher commercial loads, and the negative environment of wall to wall political advertising, not an environment that most general market advertisers are comfortable with. The local TV broadcasters were able to make up for this delayed brand advertising with political advertising. Unfortunately, we did not make up that deficit. Third, we are seeing core radio buying transitioning faster to programmatic impacting our multiplatform group ahead of our ability to fully participate in the programmatic TAM with the rollout of our Audiograph platform.

In fact, according to Ad Exchange, for a leading technology industry publication, the percentage of advertising agencies that buy audio programmatically has grown from 41% in 2022 to 82% this year. I will get into more specifics on the current status of our Audiograph rollout in a few minutes. In addition to programmatic, targeting measurability, and attribution have historically separated broadcast radio from digital media and are increasingly important elements for all advertisers. Not just those using programmatic. We are solving for this with our Audiograph platform. Fourth, as mentioned in our Q2 earnings call, we are not immune to macroeconomic issues, and in particular, consumer facing businesses where fuel volatility and broader inflation are pressuring advertisers' budgets.

These macroeconomic issues also contributed to consumer uncertainty. Which according to the World Economic Policy Uncertainty Index, recently spiked to an all time high, dramatically higher than the 9/11, financial crisis, and even the COVID 19 periods. The combination of all time high consumer uncertainty and macroeconomic pressures certainly had an impact on the multiplatform group's negative financial performance this quarter And despite the strong momentum of our digital and podcast groups this quarter, may even have moderated some of their performance as well. We expect this to continue through the remainder of the year compounded by additional uncertainty resulting from the midterm elections.

The audio and media services group, which includes our Katz TV, Katz Radio, and RCS businesses, had another strong quarter. Revenue was $82 million up 22.6% year over year, driven primarily by television political as well as the growth of digital audio and video revenues. Excluding the impact of political revenue, the audio and media services group's revenue, was still up 2.9%. Adjusted EBITDA $37 million up 65.9% compared to the prior year. And we have continued to grow adjusted EBITDA over time with a focus on an increasingly meaningful digital business and operating efficiencies.

Given the accelerating adoption of audio programmatic and the resulting impact on the multiplatform group, Audiograph is more critical than ever to our long term success. You heard us discuss our Audiograph platform on past calls, Simply put, with Audiograph, we are meeting the average advertising community the way they want to buy advertising. Given the limited reach of the ad supported digital audio players, we believe broadcast radio is the only solution for buyers to get the audience reach they need for audio campaigns. Advertisers will also be able to access the full suite of our audio products, from broadcast radio to streaming to podcasting in 1 offering.

As a result, we believe that Audiograph has the potential to meaningfully improve the performance of our broadcast radio business. Additionally, all the data work associated with Audiograph helps us in providing improved measurement, targeting, and attribution for our non programmatic advertising as well, closing that gap between broadcast radio and digital media. As we look at the financial opportunity for our programmatic revenue, our expectation is that we will hit our guidance of $200 million of programmatic revenue for the year and that in 2027, total programmatic revenue will be approximately $400 million approximately $100 million of that total being incremental broadcast programmatic revenue. Furthermore, we are excited about the potential of AgenTic buying.

Including new capabilities developed by our Triton digital ad tech platform that enabling AI agents to transact directly across the audio advertising ecosystem. Triton recently completed its first agent to agent media buy, automating the process from campaign request and negotiation through booking and demonstrating the potential to make audio substantially easier and more efficient for advertisers to buy. As I mentioned in my opening, I also want to discuss an important change in how we plan to manage and report our business beginning next year. In 2027, we plan to separate our podcasting business from the Digital Audio Group and report them as separate stand alone segments.

This additional granularity is expected to make it easier for the investor community to fully value all 4 of our segments and our entire company. 3 of our expected 4 future segments are strong growth businesses, and we think audiograph and our attribution tools will be an important component to getting our multiplatform group back to growth. Additionally, we are already working on plans for incremental cost efficiencies in 2027,, which include leveraging the strategic deployment of AI and other technology solutions to enhance efficiencies through the organization as we have done in the past.

As we continue to execute on our strategic goals and opportunities, Richard and I are committed to allocating and optimizing the company's assets to create value for our stakeholders, including improvement of our capital structure and positioning the company for future growth. And with that, I will turn it over to Richard.

P3 Health (PIII) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Adjusted EBITDA profit stretched to a third straight quarter as guidance rose.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

P3 Health Partners Inc. (PIII +6.25%) achieved its third consecutive quarter of Adjusted EBITDA profitability, reporting $20.9 million for the third quarter. Management stated that the results were driven by improved payer economics, disciplined medical cost management, and favorable prior period settlements. The company raised its full-year Adjusted EBITDA guidance midpoint to $100 million while identifying a multiyear pipeline of value creation opportunities. Management noted that operational focus remains on increasing density in existing markets and executing a capital-efficient entry strategy in new geographies such as Nebraska, while teams work to mitigate increased medical cost pressure observed in the third quarter.

Operator: Please note, this event is being recorded. I would now like to turn the conference over to William Hoover with Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

William Hoover: Thank you, operator, and thank you for joining us today. Before we proceed with the call, I would like to remind everyone that certain statements made during this call are forward-looking statements under the U.S. Federal Securities Laws, including statements regarding our financial outlook and long-term target. These forward-looking statements are only predictions and are based largely on our current expectations and projections about the future events and financial trends that we believe may affect our business, financial conditions, and results of operations. These statements are subject to risks and uncertainties that could cause actual results to differ materially from historical experience or present expectations.

Additional information concerning factors that could cause actual results to differ from statements made on this call is contained in our periodic reports filed with the SEC. The forward-looking statements made during this call speak only as of the date hereof, and the company undertakes no obligation to update or revise these forward-looking statements. We will refer to certain non-GAAP financial measures on this call, including adjusted operating expense, Adjusted EBITDA, Adjusted EBITDA per member per month, normalized Adjusted EBITDA, medical margin, medical margin per member per month, and cash flow. These non-GAAP financial measures are in addition to and not a substitute for or superior to the measures of financial performance prepared in accordance with GAAP.

There are a number of limitations related to the use of these non-GAAP financial measures. For example, other companies may calculate similarly titled non-GAAP financial measures differently. Please refer to the appendix of our earnings release for a reconciliation of these non-GAAP financial measures to the most directly comparable GAAP measures. Information presented on this call is contained in the press release that we issued today and in our SEC filings, which may be accessed from the Investor page of the P3 Health Partners website. I will now turn the call over to Aric Coffman, CEO of P3 Health Partners.

Aric Coffman: Good afternoon, and thank you for joining us today to discuss our third quarter results. It has been two and a half years since I began leading P3, and the changes we have made across the business continue to build on one another. Our third quarter results reflect that work, with $21 million of Adjusted EBITDA, bringing our year-to-date Adjusted EBITDA to $101 million. As a result, we are raising the low end of our full-year Adjusted EBITDA guidance to $90 million, increasing the midpoint to $100 million. Our focus now is on building upon the durable foundation we have created.

We have four areas of focus: medical cost management, quality and burden of illness documentation, disciplined growth, and refining our operations through people, process, and technology. I want to thank our teams across the country for the focus and care they bring every day, and our provider and payer partners who share our commitment to delivering better care for the patients we serve. There are two areas of the business I want to highlight that drive our results: operational execution and growth. I'll start with our execution. First and foremost, our year-to-date Medicare Advantage medical expense is 0.7% below the full-year 2025 medical expense on a normalized basis.

In other words, this means we are bending the medical cost curve through the first three quarters of the year. Our combined approach to medical cost management, quality execution, and accurate documentation create a compounding impact. With peers in our sector reporting medical cost trend of 7% or more, this is a meaningful differentiator for P3. This quarter, we showed progress across our model in the following areas. Utilization management had its strongest quarter of the year, with meaningful gains in how we manage both inpatient stays and outpatient services. Compared to Q1, our inpatient to observation rate is up more than 10 points, and outpatient prior authorization avoidance has improved 68%.

Quality had its strongest quarter of the year, with our highest weighted outcome measures moving in the right direction. We saw nine-point improvements over the prior year in both blood pressure control and control of diabetes, which are two triple-weighted measures. Patient and provider engagement remains ahead of plan, with our teams having seen 91% of our patients year to date and 97% of high-risk members. We are enhancing our AI-enabled technology to improve the point-of-care experience for clinicians and improve outreach capabilities to our patients. In addition, we continue to invest in our P3 Restore program, aimed at directly supporting our clinician partners. Clinical leadership and analytics continue to deepen.

We built an enhanced medical economics function focused on more quickly identifying areas of higher cost and utilization. That then allows us to put interventions in place against the findings. Additionally, we continue to invest in talent and are thrilled to welcome Dr. Mike Millie as Chief Clinical Officer to lead and strengthen our clinical execution. Turning to growth, our growth comes from two sources: expanding in the markets where we already operate and entering new markets in a capital-efficient and deliberate way. First, expansion in existing markets. We grow our legacy footprint in three ways: new plans in counties adjacent to our current service areas, increasing provider density, and taking on more delegated functions with our current payer partners.

This growth runs on infrastructure, care teams, and relationships we already have in place, so it adds membership at low incremental cost. Each function we take on also gives us better returns by controlling medical costs and quality outcomes more tightly. Second is smart growth in new geographies. New market entry is a repeatable model, partnership, development, and then a move to meaningful risk on a glide path. We start with delegated operations for plan partners and building the clinical and operational infrastructure. Then we expand the scope of delegated functions and finally move to full-risk arrangements.

This sequence keeps growth capital-light and gives us time to build network relationships to understand the local market before we take on full risk. Nebraska is the model for this approach. Since launching in May, we have put utilization management, payment integrity, coding, and quality programs in place. On top of those functions, we have added our clinical programs, including Senior Wellness Centers that provide direct patient care, more points of access, and high-risk clinical programs. Demand for this model is strong. There are more than 60 mid-sized Medicare Advantage plans facing similar pressure on medical cost and quality. Our solution manages more of the levers that drive performance with a clear path to margin first and membership growth after.

We are actively pursuing opportunities in our pipeline, sequencing them at a pace our infrastructure supports, and on terms that reward the value we create. Overall, our third quarter results reinforce the consistency of the earnings improvement we have established this year, and we enter the final quarter with momentum and a stronger operating foundation. The core levers that drive our performance are demonstrating measurable results, and we continue to see substantial opportunity within our existing book of business. We have identified more than $200 million of potential value creation across payer economics, clinical execution, and operating efficiency. As we execute on these opportunities, we expect them to translate into material earnings growth and more consistent financial performance.

We intend to provide greater clarity on the timing, expected realization, and earnings contribution of these opportunities early in 2027. With that, I'll turn the call over to Amir to discuss our clinical performance.

Amir Bacchus: Thank you, Aric. Our year-to-date medical cost performance continues to compare well against the industry. As expected, the third quarter medical expense ran higher than the first half of the year, driven primarily by outpatient and professional services, while inpatient costs tracked in line with expectations. Within outpatient, cardiovascular procedures are the largest driver. Our response, built on the care enablement model, has two parts: targeting the specific drivers of the increase and continuing to strengthen the programs behind our year-to-date performance. First, we are going directly after those drivers. Our teams have identified where costs are rising and are focused on mitigating them in the fourth quarter and into next year.

One of our most effective tools is our high utilizer model, which identifies members with repeat hospital, emergency, and observation visits down to the provider they see. Our clinicians and care managers step in earlier and direct care to the appropriate site. We are focused on transitions of care, including medication reviews and care coordination for patients being discharged from a facility. For those patients with barriers to care, our teams are performing at-home assessments to reach high-risk patients who are not seeing their primary care physician. Second, utilization management has its strongest quarter of the year. Since the first quarter, our outpatient prior authorization effectiveness has improved 68% per member.

On the inpatient side, our review teams raised the rate at which appropriate hospital stays moved to observation from 24% to 34%. Third, we continue to execute on quality. We are submitting supplemental data at more than twice last year's pace, and our quality outreach reached more than 76,000 members, or 96% of our MA members in the quarter. That work is showing up where it matters most for patient outcomes. Blood pressure control and diabetes control, two of our most heavily weighted measures, are each up approximately nine points over last year. Better control of these measures means better control of overall medical spend and improvement in outcomes.

Finally, we continue to invest in our clinician partners through P3 Restore. In the third quarter, we added 23 clinicians to the program's one-on-one coaching and education program. Their feedback continues to be strong, especially around work-life balance, leadership, and a renewed sense of purpose. On the backdrop of provider burnout, about 40%, and a physician shortage, this is important for long-term success. Clinical foundation behind our year-to-date performance is in place and scaling. Under the leadership of Dr. Mike Millie, our new Chief Clinical Officer, our teams are focused on the levers that provide the highest quality care to patients at the lowest cost to the system, and that focus will carry through the fourth quarter and into 2027.

With that, I'll turn the call over to Leif.

Leif Pedersen: Thank you, Amir, and good afternoon. Q3 marked our third consecutive profitable quarter. We delivered $21 million of Adjusted EBITDA. Two items drove a meaningful part of that result. First, approximately $7 million of favorable medical margin from the 2025 CMS ACO reconciliation. Second, approximately $5 million of non-cash income from a liability release associated with our ACO joint venture. Excluding these and other prior period and non-recurring items, underlying Q3 Adjusted EBITDA was approximately $10 million. Importantly, the underlying business remained profitable despite medical expense pressure in the quarter. Year-to-date performance continues to reflect improved payer economics, strong clinical execution, and progress in utilization management and payment integrity.

Based on our year-to-date results and current expectations for the fourth quarter, we are raising our midpoint guidance from $95 million to $100 million. I'll cover three areas today: our Q3 financial performance, liquidity, and our outlook for the remainder of 2026. Starting with membership, we ended the third quarter with approximately 105,000 at-risk members, compared to approximately 116,000 in Q3 of 2025. The year-over-year decline primarily reflects deliberate portfolio actions taken in 2025 and early in 2026 to exit or reduce exposure to arrangements that did not meet our economic thresholds. As a result, our current membership base is more concentrated in payer and provider relationships where we believe the P3 model can generate attractive and sustainable economics.

In addition to our at-risk membership, we manage approximately 28,000 lives through management services arrangements, creating a potential pipeline for deeper future at-risk relationships and bringing total lives under management to approximately 133,000. Moving to the top line, third quarter revenue was approximately $396 million, compared with $345 million in the prior year quarter, a 15% increase. Capitated revenue per at-risk member increased approximately 18% year-over-year, reflecting rate progression, improved payer economics, and continued maturation of our burden of illness performance. Total revenue also benefited from growth in other revenue streams, primarily management services and incentive-related revenue, along for fee-for-service and care coordination activity. Against that revenue backdrop, medical expense was the most impactful operating trend in the quarter.

Medical claims expense was approximately $322 million. The quarter included favorable prior period adjustments associated with the 2025 CMS ACO reconciliation noted earlier. Year-to-date, Medicare Advantage Part A and B medical expense remains approximately 0.7% below the full year 2025 level on a normalized basis. However, medical expense stepped up during the quarter compared to the first half, particularly across outpatient and professional services. Improving that trend is one of our primary operating priorities as we finish the year. As Amir described, we are focused on specific areas driving the increase through utilization management, care management, payment integrity, and targeted Med Econ work. Medical margin for the quarter was approximately $44 million, or $140 Per Member Per Month.

Medical loss ratio was approximately 90.6% after adjusting for the favorable prior period items recognized during the quarter. While medical expense increased during the quarter, our year-to-date economics continued to benefit from improved payer funding, utilization management, and payer payment integrity processes, and disciplined clinical execution. Beyond medical costs, we continue to manage the overall cost structure closely. Adjusted operating expense in the quarter was approximately $35 million, compared to approximately $32 million in the prior quarter. The increase reflects continued investment in our data and clinical capabilities and the build-out of our Nebraska market infrastructure. We continue to invest in capabilities that directly support medical costs, quality, and burden of illness performance.

At the same time, we are reducing back office and third-party spend to keep our cost structure aligned with the size and needs of the business. Bringing those components together, Q3 Adjusted EBITDA was $21 million, compared with an Adjusted EBITDA loss of approximately $46 million in the third quarter of 2025. As outlined earlier, underlying Adjusted EBITDA for the quarter was approximately $10 million. On a year-to-date basis, Adjusted EBITDA was $101 million, compared with Adjusted EBITDA loss of approximately $85 million through the same period in 2025. After excluding prior period items and specific non-recurring operating costs, underlying Adjusted EBITDA was approximately $29 million. For us, that is an important measure of the progress we have made.

The underlying business has generated positive EBITDA in each of the first three quarters of 2026, giving a stronger operating base as we move through the fourth quarter and into 2027. With that, let me move to liquidity. We ended the quarter with approximately $44 million of cash and cash equivalents. We continue to actively manage liquidity and capital requirements against our operating plan while maintaining the investments necessary to improve the underlying performance of the business and preserve appropriate financial flexibility as we move into 2027. That brings me to our outlook for the balance of the year. We are raising the low end of our full year 2026 Adjusted EBITDA guidance from $80 million to $90 million.

Resulting in a revised range of $90 million-$110 million, and increasing the midpoint from $95 million to $100 million. The revised outlook reflects our year-to-date reported performance, including favorable prior period items recognized during the year, as well as our current expectations for the underlying business in the fourth quarter. Our outlook incorporates the higher medical cost run rate experienced in Q3 and our current expectations across revenue, medical expense, and operating costs. It also assumes continued execution against the clinical utilization management, payment integrity, and cost initiatives we have discussed today.

Our priorities for the remainder of the year remain clear: improve Medicare Advantage medical cost performance, maintain discipline around operating expense, continue strengthening payer economics, and build on positive underlying earnings performance generated through the first three quarters. With that, I'll turn it back to Aric.

Aric Coffman: Thanks, Leif. Before we open the line for questions, I want to leave you with three takeaways. First, our clinical and operational execution is delivering. The structural and clinical work we set out to do two years ago is now embedded in how we run the business. Utilization management and quality have their strongest quarters of the year, our clinical programs continue to scale, and our differentiated medical cost trend remains below what peers are reporting. Second, we are growing in a smart and balanced way. We are deepening the partnerships in our existing markets. Nebraska is proving our framework in a new market, and the opportunity to bring that framework to more health plans is significant.

We will pursue it at a pace our infrastructure supports and on terms that reward the value we create. Lastly, 2026 has set the baseline for 2027 and beyond. Our year-to-date results and an upward revision to full-year guidance midpoint reflect what our teams have built over the last two years. Combined with the $200 million of value creation we have identified, our performance reinforces our confidence in the growth and earnings potential of P3. With that, operator, please open the line for questions.

Leif Pedersen: Hey, Ryan. Leif, let me take a first stab at it, and I'll let Aric and Amir kind of augment where necessary.
