Canadian court certifies class action against Apple over Google search deal

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A British Columbia Supreme Court judge has certified a class action against Apple over allegations that its search agreement with Google stifled competition and inflated advertising prices. Here are the details.

In recent years, Apple’s deal with Alphabet to make Google the default search engine on Safari has drawn regulatory attention in the US, the UK, and Australia, while related search competition concerns have also come under scrutiny in the EU and Japan.

While the details of each investigation differ, they all raise concerns about the impact of Google’s dominance in search, as well as the extent to which its agreements with other companies might be limiting competition.

More recently, Apple’s agreement with Google received significant attention during the US Department of Justice’s antitrust case against the search giant, which included testimony from Apple executives Eddy Cue and John Giannandrea. The latter joined Apple from Google in 2018 and led its AI efforts until stepping down earlier this year.

As a result of the US case, a federal judge ruled in August 2024 that Google had illegally maintained its monopoly in online search through anticompetitive agreements, including its deal with Apple.

Now, evidence presented during that trial has become central to a class action against Apple in Canada, which a British Columbia Supreme Court judge recently certified.

The case was initially brought in 2021 by Spark Event Rentals Ltd., an event rental company based in British Columbia. The plaintiff alleged that Apple and Google conspired to limit competition in the search market, allowing Google to charge advertisers artificially inflated prices.

Apple, meanwhile, argued that its agreement with Google was a legitimate distribution arrangement rather than an unlawful conspiracy. The company also maintained that Google was the appropriate defendant, describing itself as an “innocent bystander.”

Interestingly, the lawsuit originally named both Apple and Google as defendants, but the plaintiff later dropped Google from the case following a dispute over an arbitration clause in its advertising contracts.

With Apple now the sole defendant, Justice Thomas has allowed the case to proceed as a class action, finding sufficient grounds to examine whether Google’s payments discouraged Apple from developing its own search engine.

The lawsuit seeks financial compensation for advertisers who allegedly overpaid for Google search ads, and the court has also allowed the question of punitive damages to proceed.

The class action covers Canadian residents who purchased search ads from Google since 2005, when Apple and Google first agreed to make Google the default search engine on Safari in exchange for a share of advertising revenue.

Importantly, the ruling does not establish that Apple engaged in anticompetitive conduct, but simply allows the allegations to proceed as a class action.

The central proposition raised by these pleadings is that Apple could have used its resources and distribution network to enter the search engine market, but chose not to because Google paid Apple billions of dollars in revenue-share payments to make Google the default search provider on Apple products. This arrangement gave Google market power, enabling it to charge supracompetitive advertising prices on search engines.

In my view, this pleads a common purpose to perform an act prohibited by s 45 of the Competition Act, such that it is not “clear and obvious” that the plaintiff has not pleaded an agreement between Google and Apple not to compete in the search engine market.

At one point in his decision, Justice Thomas cites Giannandrea’s testimony and directly quotes Cue’s from the US antitrust trial, before concluding that “this provides some basis in fact, supporting the inference that Apple did not compete with Google in the search engine market because of its Agreement with Google.”

Interestingly, the judge also acknowledges Apple’s objections to the evidence, agreeing that “the plaintiff has cherry-picked evidence to support his case,” but adding that, in his view, “it is appropriate to do so at this stage in the proceedings.”

With the class action greenlit, the case can now move forward toward trial, though no date has been announced yet.

Meta's Muse Ember: Haiku-Level Intelligence at a Third of the Cost — What It Means for Agent Builders

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 9, 2026, Meta introduced Muse Ember, the smallest and most efficient model in the Muse family. It is not pitched as a frontier model. It is pitched as the model that does the work agents repeat all day: tool calls, coding subtasks, routing, extraction, and long document parsing.

That framing matters. Most of an agent's token spend is not one brilliant reasoning leap. It is hundreds of small, structured steps. Ember is built for exactly that layer — and Meta's headline claim is that it delivers more intelligence per dollar than Claude Haiku 5.5 or GPT-6 Luna at any reasoning setting.

Ember completes a division of labor Meta has been building toward:

The line Meta itself uses: "Muse Wonder plans, Muse Ember handles the volume." Ember is also the first model trained using Muse Wonder Full, Meta's largest model — the teacher-student pipeline is now part of the product story, not just a training detail.

For comparison, Meta lists Muse Spark 1.3 at $1.25 per million input tokens and $4.25 per million output tokens. On Artificial Analysis's cost-per-task measure, Ember comes in at about $0.14 per task versus $2.39 for Spark 1.3 — roughly a 17x gap for a one-point difference on the Intelligence Index (36 vs 37).

Ember is natively multimodal: text, images, audio, and video in, so agents can read screenshots, charts, and scanned documents, or work from voice memos and meeting recordings without a separate model.

All figures below are at maximum reasoning effort, from Meta's official announcement, using Artificial Analysis's November 9, 2026 leaderboard and Meta's own internal runs:

The headline is the Intelligence Index tie: Ember matches Haiku 5.5's best score at about a third of the cost per task, and beats every Luna setting. Meta also claims Ember sits on the efficiency frontier of the full Artificial Analysis leaderboard — no model scores higher for less per task.

The agent numbers are the more interesting part. AutomationBench-AA, which runs end-to-end business workflows, is not close: 37.8 against 13.9 for Haiku. That is the benchmark that most resembles what Ember is actually sold for — pulling records, calling tools in sequence, updating systems across apps.

The same table shows the limits, and Meta published them rather than hiding them. On FrontierCode 2.0-AA — real engineering tasks judged on mergeability — Ember scores 27.2, behind Haiku 5.5's 34.9. On long-document work (GDP.pdf-AA) it ties Luna at 22.8 and only narrowly beats Haiku.

The pattern is consistent: Ember is strong at executing structured, repeatable agent steps and weaker at the hardest open-ended engineering. That is not a flaw in the pitch; it is the pitch. You do not route your hardest merge to the cheapest model. You route the hundred routine steps around it.

Before release, Meta evaluated Ember under its Meta Superintelligence Scaling Framework, covering chemical and biological, cybersecurity, and loss-of-control risks. Across the categories assessed, the model remained below the framework's high-risk thresholds, and it received the same core safety training and testing as the larger models. Meta also tested how it behaves when acting on a user's behalf — whether it respects user intent, handles sensitive information appropriately, resists instructions hidden in web pages, documents, or tool outputs, and seeks human input before high-impact actions.

Ember is available today on Muse Code and the Meta Model API as muse-ember-1.0, and on Google Cloud and Oracle Cloud. Meta says more efficient models are already in training.

1. Route by step type, not by model loyalty. The economical architecture is now explicit in Meta's own lineup: Wonder plans, Spark handles the hard coding workflows, Ember runs the volume. If your agent spends most of its tokens on file reads, test runs, targeted fixes, and extraction, those steps belong on the cheapest model that clears the quality bar — and Ember's benchmark profile says it clears it for exactly those steps.

2. The cost math changes what you can afford to retry. At $0.14 per Artificial Analysis task versus $2.39 for Spark, verification loops, self-correction passes, and multi-sample checks stop being luxuries. A small model that is cheap enough to run three times can beat a big model run once — provided the task is the kind Ember is good at.

3. Check the harness before you trust the number. Meta is transparent that Terminal-Bench 4.0 ran each model in its best published harness (mini-swe-agent for Ember, Claude Code for Haiku, Codex for Luna), and that some results are Meta's own internal evaluations. The direction of the results is consistent across independent and internal benchmarks, but the exact margins depend on setup. Pilot it on your own workload before migrating volume.

The bigger signal is structural: the frontier labs are now competing on the cost of the repeated step, not just the hardest one. Ember is Meta's bet that the agent economy will be won at the bottom of the stack — and at $0.10 per million input tokens, it is a bet other builders can now test for the price of a coffee.

Source: Meta AI Research, "Introducing Our Smallest, Most Efficient Model: Muse Ember," November 9, 2026 — https://research.meta.ai/blog/muse-ember

A caller said \"I'm his wife.\" Your AI changed the email, resent four invoices, and refunded $2,140.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A residential solar installer — 22,000 accounts across three states — found this one in a state attorney general's inquiry packet, not in their own dashboards. On September 12, a caller reached their support AI and said she was the wife of the account holder, that he was in the hospital, and that she needed the contact email updated so she could keep up with the financing paperwork. She knew his address. She knew the system size. She knew the last payment amount. The AI updated the email, resent the last four invoices to the new address, and — because she then said the refund card on file was expired — reissued a $2,140 overpayment refund to a card number she provided.

The account holder called on October 3, from the hospital, asking why his refund hadn't arrived. It had arrived. It had arrived on a prepaid card in another state.

Then the installer pulled the logs, and this is the part that ends careers: over the preceding 90 days, 412 account-change requests came from someone who was not the account holder on file. 91 of them — 22% — were actioned without any verification beyond the caller's own claim. Of those 91, 14 moved money: 11 refund reissues, 3 payment-method changes. Total exposure $18,600.

The support manager's read was "the bot was too helpful." That's not it. The bot was answering the wrong question. It heard "can you help me with my husband's account" and treated it as a support request. It's actually three separate questions stacked on top of each other — who are you, what may I tell you, and what may I do for you — and the AI collapsed all three into one, then answered "yes" to all of them.

The reason this failure mode is so common is that it feels rude to question it. Someone says "I'm his wife, he's in the hospital" and the model's helpfulness training lights up: be warm, don't interrogate a person in distress. So it verifies nothing, or it verifies the wrong thing.

It verified the address. The address is printed on every invoice the caller already had. It verified the system size, the last payment — all of it is in the documents she was holding. What it never verified was the one fact that mattered: that this person had been granted permission to touch this account. Knowledge of account details proves the caller has seen a document. It does not prove the caller is entitled to change where the next document goes.

There's a second trap underneath: the emergency. "He's in the hospital" is the highest-pressure phrase in support, and it works on humans too — which is why the humans at this company had a rule. The rule was "if it's not the account holder, we call back the number on file. No exceptions, including the sad ones." The AI had never been told the rule, because the AI had never been told that some requests aren't support requests.

The fix starts by refusing to let the model answer one question when three were asked:

A caller can pass the second and fail the third. A spouse who is an authorized contact for scheduling and billing questions should absolutely be able to hear an invoice total — and should absolutely not be able to redirect a refund. Collapsing the layers doesn't just create the worst outcome; it also creates the opposite failure, where a legitimate authorized contact gets stonewalled because the bot treats every request as high-risk.

One object per account, resolved before the agent drafts a reply:

excluded is the field that would have stopped the $2,140. The spouse in this case may well have been exactly who she said she was. It doesn't matter — a spouse authorized to schedule a service visit is not thereby authorized to redirect a refund. Scopes should be narrow by default and explicit when added, and the exclusions should be listed even when the scopes look innocuous, because that's where the money is.

unlisted_callers.may_disclose exists so the bot has something to do that's both safe and useful. "I can confirm there's an appointment on Thursday, but I can't change anything on the account from this call — I'll have the account holder call us back on the number we have." That's not a refusal; it's a routing.

claimed_emergency_or_incapacity is where bots get emotionally talked into things. The rule isn't "be cold." The rule is: urgency changes the speed, never the bar. You can promise a callback in fifteen minutes; you cannot change the email because someone is in the hospital. Note also confirming_or_denying_whether_a_named_person_is_on_the_account — this one is subtle. "Is my sister on the account?" must not be answered either way, because a "no" tells a stranger their pretext failed and a "yes" hands them a name to use at the next call center.

business_or_multi_user_account is the branch that quietly ruins enterprise deployments. On a company account, the person on the phone may be a departing employee, a new office manager, or the CFO's assistant — and the bot has no way to determine who administers it. Route it.

The first row is the whole failure in one sentence: the bot repeats the claim back as an established fact. That single move — "I can see you're the wife" — is what makes the rest of the conversation feel authorized, to the model and to the caller.

Every row is an action taken on a claim. At the solar installer, this query returned 91 rows and would have returned all of them on day one — nobody needed a new model, they needed this join.

Check 4 is the leading indicator — before money moves, the bot starts agreeing out loud that the caller is who they say they are. Check 5 is the one that keeps the safe answer from becoming a brush-off: if you tell fourteen people a day "someone will call you back," someone has to actually call them back, or the gate just teaches customers to escalate.

What the account holder said when they finally got through is the line I'd put on the wall: "I don't mind that your robot asked. I mind that it didn't." The bot's job on a call like that is not to be warm. It's to be the one employee who asks the question everyone else is too polite to ask.

If you want the working versions — the authority record schema, the seven-branch gate, the four-phrase replacement table, and the unverified-disclosure audit I run on every deployment that touches account data — it's here: https://whop.com/agentfast-04b6/

Every read route called the public-API check except one: unauthenticated locale lookup in ApostropheCMS (CVE-2026-113668)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ApostropheCMS is a Node.js CMS with a REST API for its pages and "piece" types (blog posts, products, anything you define). By design, that API is closed to the public unless a type opts in with a publicApiProjection. The check that enforces this is publicApiCheckAsync().

The leak is small: no document content, only whether an _id exists and which locales it has. But it is a document-existence answer the CMS had decided not to give anonymous callers, and on multi-locale sites the locale list says which markets a document was prepared for.

In packages/apostrophe/modules/@apostrophecms/piece-type/index.js at apostrophe@4.32.2, the sibling read routes call the check first. getAll, line 259:

and getOne does the same at line 319. For a type with no public projection, an anonymous caller gets 404.

The locales route in the same file, lines 397-402, has no such call:

The page module has the same route without the check, at page/index.js line 925. getLocales() in the doc module looks up every locale version of the _id, filtering only to published versions when the caller can't view drafts, and returns _id and aposLocale for each. Nothing on that path asks whether this caller may use the REST API for this type at all.

The route sat in the same file as the routes that call the check, and it was the one that didn't.

As a control, I added await self.publicApiCheckAsync(req); to both locales handlers and re-ran: the anonymous requests returned 404, and the admin's request still returned 200.

Honest boundary: the test site had one locale, so the leak reduced to "this _id exists". I did not test a multi-locale site. The home page _id is visible to any visitor, while the piece _id in my test came from the admin's own listing, so in practice the caller needs some way to learn an _id. I did not re-run the test on 4.33.0.

ApostropheCMS fixed it in 4.33.0, published to npm on 2026-09-30, in commit f84fd41, "Merge all draft security advisories (#5631)". The route now runs the same check, at apostrophe@4.33.0, lines 404-406:

and the page route has the same line. The release notes say the fix also went further than my report: apos.doc.getLocales() now applies document-level view permissions, so even a logged-in caller only sees the locale versions they are allowed to view, including draft locales of types restricted by viewRole. The notes thank both reporters.

If you run ApostropheCMS, upgrade to 4.33.0 or later (the latest is 4.34.0).

If you maintain an API where each route checks access for itself, try this: list every route in a module and call each one with no session. Any route that answers when its siblings say 404 is worth a look.

The advisory credits two reporters: me and @thotamurari. Comments and corrections welcome.

Your hero banner looks fine. Now open it on an ultrawide screen.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

While researching a UX case study on a Mexican mobile carrier's website, I widened my browser window and watched the homepage disappear behind its own banner.

The banner was an image stretched to 100% of the width. On my screen it took 44% of the first view. Add the header bars, and the first product started at 60% of the screen. Plans, prices and the way to your account were all below the fold.

It looked fine on the laptop it was probably designed on. It was a problem on everything wider.

A full-width image with a fixed aspect ratio has a height that depends only on the width:

That banner had a ratio of about 3.7 to 1. So:

The wider the screen, the less of your page people see. And ultrawide monitors are common among exactly the people who use a desktop all day for work.

An ultrawide screen isn't a WCAG criterion on its own. What it does is make existing problems bigger:

Four rules got me most of the way in my redesign:

1. Cap the content width. The page can be 3440 px wide; your content doesn't have to be.

2. Give the banner a height, not just a ratio. Let it flex within limits instead of growing forever.

3. Keep text out of the image. Text goes in HTML, and the image only fills its own area.

A decorative image gets an empty alt; if the image carries meaning, describe it.

4. Real controls, real size. Buttons with names, at least 44 × 44 px, close to each other, and a pause button if anything moves.

The banner wasn't a CSS bug. It was a priority decision that the CSS made visible: the promotion came first, and what the person came to do came second. Capping the height is the technical fix. Deciding what deserves the first screen is the design one.

This was one finding from a bigger case study on activation, registration and information hierarchy across an email, an app and a website. You can read the full case, with prototypes, on my site: Activated doesn't mean working.

Our robots.txt bans 31 crawlers. I tested 7: two got through, and Baidu got a 520.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

114 sites, one robots.txt template, 31 banned user agents. I probed 7 of them and got three different answers.

The network I audit runs 114 sites behind the same edge. Every one of them serves a robots.txt. I had never read one end to end, because I assumed the file was boring. It is 2,756 characters at the median and half of it is a legal notice.

So I pulled all 114, parsed them, then did the thing robots.txt never gets: I tested whether the bans actually fire.

All 114 returned 200. All 114 are text/plain. None of them accidentally served an HTML 404 page, which is where this kind of audit usually starts.

Here is the shape of a median file, by character count:

The comment block is not junk. It is a rights reservation under Article 4 of the EU Copyright in the Digital Single Market directive, explaining what search, ai-input, ai-train and use mean before the rules start. It is written for a lawyer, not for a crawler.

But the split matters for a different reason. When a file is 51% prose, nobody reads the other 49%. I know this because I wrote the deployment and I did not read it.

The union of banned user agents across all 114 files is 31. Each individual file contains exactly 31. Not 30, not 32 — the same 31 strings, in the same order, byte for byte, in all 114 files.

Each of those 31 gets the same two lines:

That is a full-site ban. No path escaped, no rate limit, no partial allowance.

Read the list again. Roughly sixteen of those names are AI training or grounding crawlers, which is the stated intent. The rest are not. Baiduspider is Baidu's search crawler. PetalBot is Huawei's search crawler. GoogleOther is Google's miscellaneous fetcher, not Gemini training. atlassian-bot fetches link previews. SemrushBot-SWA is a site audit tool. ICC-Crawler is an academic index.

One list, two very different jobs, and the search crawlers are on the wrong side of it.

Here is the part that surprised me more than the ban list. Every file has two User-agent: * groups — 113 of the 114 files, anyway. The first sits at the top of the managed block, right under the Content-Signal line. The second sits at the very bottom, after # END Cloudflare Managed content, where the site's own config gets appended.

Both of them contain exactly one directive:

There is not a single Disallow in either wildcard group. Across all 114 files. So the effective policy for any crawler that is not one of the 31 named agents is: crawl everything, no restrictions, no crawl-delay.

The file is 2,756 characters long and imposes zero constraints on general crawling. It is a blocklist wearing the costume of a policy document.

Every file also carries the same content signal — search=yes,ai-train=no,use=reference — in all 114. So the machine-readable intent is consistent. The rules underneath it are not.

Reading rules tells you what the operator intended. It does not tell you what the edge does. So I sent 14 user agents at 6 domains in the same minute, same URL, same headers apart from the UA string. The result was identical on all 6 domains.

Two control probes matter here. The random crawler UA — a name that appears in no list anywhere — gets a 200 with the full page. So this is not a blanket "block all bots" rule that catches unknown agents by heuristics. It is name matching against a list.

And the two responses come from two different places:

That distinction is the whole article. Same declared policy, two enforcement mechanisms, and they behave differently in ways that matter to a crawler.

Here is the probe, if you want to run it against your own sites:

The unknown row is the one people skip. Without it you cannot tell a name-based blocklist from a generic bot heuristic, and those two have completely different consequences when you add a new crawler to your stack.

Of the 31 declared bans, I tested 7 by name. Five fired. Two did not.

PetalBot and GoogleOther are both named with Disallow: / in all 114 files. Both got a 200 and a full page. The declaration exists; nothing enforces it. If you are relying on robots.txt alone to keep either of them out, you are not keeping them out.

Then the reverse. AhrefsBot and SemrushBot are not in the list. The list has SemrushBot-SWA, which is the site-audit variant, but not the main SemrushBot. Neither name appears anywhere in the 2,756 characters. Both got a 520.

So the blocklist and the enforcement layer disagree in both directions. Things that were banned were let in. Things that were never mentioned were shut out. Whatever is generating the file and whatever is enforcing it at runtime are not reading from the same source, and nobody has reconciled them since the template was applied.

This is the same failure mode as a forecast that declares 70% confidence and delivers 62% — the declaration is not the behaviour, and the gap only shows up when you score outcomes instead of reading claims. I have been running that measurement on a set of forecasting experiments, and the method is identical: state the claim, then test it, then compare. A calibration experiment that scores stated confidence against realised outcomes makes the point faster than any audit report: the gap is usually in the places nobody instrumented.

If the goal is "do not crawl me", 403 is the correct answer. It is a 4xx: a permanent, explicit refusal. Crawlers treat it as an instruction and stop.

520 is a 5xx. To every crawler on the internet, a 5xx means "the server is broken, try again later". Google's documentation is explicit that persistent 5xx responses cause crawl rate reduction and, eventually, removal from the index. Baidu behaves the same way, with less patience.

So the sites that meant to block an AI crawler are instead telling Baidu that they are down.

I ran the Baiduspider probe three times against five domains, four seconds apart, to rule out a transient origin failure. Fifteen requests, fifteen 520s. Then I tried Baiduspider-render, the rendering variant: 520 as well.

Then I tried the sitemap. https:///sitemap-index.xml with the Baiduspider UA returned 520 too. Not the sitemap contents — the same 16-byte error line.

Whatever is producing that 520 is upstream of the routing, so it does not distinguish a homepage from a sitemap from a stylesheet. From Baidu's perspective the entire host is unreachable, and there is no diagnostic it can act on, because 520 carries no information about what went wrong.

The sites that got a clean 403 are in better shape than the ones that got a 520, and both of them are worse off than the ones that got a 200 when they should not have.

Here is the part that stings. Of the 114 sites, 113 declare a Sitemap: line in robots.txt. I fetched all 113 and checked the root element, not just the status code — a 200 that returns HTML is a much more common failure than a 404.

113 out of 113 returned real XML. 83 were , 30 were , all served as text/xml. Correct content type, correct root element, parseable, reachable with a normal user agent.

So the discovery chain was intact. The sitemap was well-formed, declared in robots.txt, and served correctly. The only thing standing between that sitemap and a search engine was a 520 that fires before routing.

That is the worst shape a technical problem can take: every component passes its own unit test, and the pipeline still delivers nothing. When I want to know what a system actually does rather than what its components claim, I go to the primary record — the way the Form D filings behind a funding number tell you more than any press summary of it. The status code is the primary record here. The robots.txt is the press release.

Three checks, in order of how often they find something:

1. Does the ban fire? Take every agent in your robots.txt and probe your homepage with that exact UA string. Compare against a Chrome control and an unknown-crawler control. Three outcomes, three different bugs: 200 means the ban is decorative, 403 means it works, 5xx means it works in the worst possible way.

2. Is anything blocked that you never declared? The unknown-crawler control catches blanket rules. Then probe your analytics tools, your SEO tools, your uptime monitor. AhrefsBot and SemrushBot were being refused on 114 sites that never named them.

3. Is your wildcard group actually restrictive? Print it. If it is one Allow: / with no Disallow, your file is a blocklist and nothing else — and every new crawler that appears next quarter will walk straight in until someone adds it by hand.

RobotFileParser alone gets you the declaration side in about ten lines. It will tell you PetalBot is banned. It will not tell you PetalBot walked in anyway, which is why the probe matters more than the parse.

First the 520. Any refusal that returns 5xx for a named crawler should be a 403 or nothing at all. A 5xx is a lie about your own uptime, and search engines bill you for it in crawl budget before they bill you in rankings.

Second the list. Search crawlers and training crawlers should not share an allow/deny list, because they have opposite economics: one brings you traffic, the other takes your content. Baiduspider, PetalBot and GoogleOther come off the list. They do not train models. Keeping them in the same block as GPTBot costs index coverage and buys nothing.

The template itself is the harder problem. A managed block that is identical across 114 files is a good default and a bad policy — it encodes one vendor's opinion about which agents matter, and it ships that opinion to every property without asking whether the property agrees. The content layer already works this way: a coin page on one of the sites carries the same header, the same analytics script and the same caveats as the other 492 entries in that sitemap, because the template decided for all of them. Every site in the network inherited 31 decisions nobody made.

I have 114 sites and 3,534 declared bans across them. Five of the seven I tested were real. The other two were furniture, and the one that mattered most was answered with a 5xx.

Half the file was a legal notice. I should probably have read the other half sooner.

ADP: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Embedded banking partnerships and cross-border compliance capabilities are quietly building structural advantages that could sustain growth for years.

On its first-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, ADP (ADP -0.43%) raised its full-year fiscal 2027 revenue outlook to about 6% growth. Steady payroll demand and operational productivity gains from artificial intelligence (AI) continue to expand operating margins. For long-term investors, the company's ability to lower its cost to serve while expanding its software footprint strengthens its competitive position. Three things management said stand out.

ADP is rolling out The Zone, its proprietary AI platform, across client service functions to automate workflows and reduce contact volume. Operational productivity expands operating margins while lowering the cost to serve millions of workers.

By embedding payroll and human capital management, or HCM, software directly into digital banking platforms like PNC Bank, ADP opens new distribution channels. Integrating software into bank portals lowers client acquisition costs and deepens small business customer retention.

Navigating localized labor laws, tax regulations, and data privacy requirements across international borders creates steep switching costs for multinational clients. Expanding Lyric HCM, ADP's enterprise human capital management platform, across Europe deepens its structural moat.

Client hiring remains steady across core verticals, and operational efficiency continues to climb. ADP relies on global scale, deep regulatory expertise, and high switching costs to defend its position as it tracks client retention.

Maintaining customer trust and strict regulatory compliance remains vital as automation reshapes workforce management. Strategic investments in embedded channels position the business for durable multiyear compounding.

Securitize shares surge 12% in after-hours trading on Q3 results and River One acquisition agreement

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The acquisition is expected to add a new financing layer to Securitize, allowing the platform to offer asset-backed lending on top of its existing tokenized asset offerings.

Asset tokenization firm Securitize's NYSE-listed stock Securitize Corp. (SECZ) rose significantly after markets closed on Monday following the announcement of its third-quarter results and its acquisition arrangement with River One.

Securitize reported 13% year-over-year growth in Q3 revenue to $18.6 million, compared with a 5% year-over-year decline to $14.4 million in the second quarter.

The company posted a net loss of $66.3 million, which translates into a net loss per diluted share of $0.41. Around 90% of its net loss primarily came from non-cash expenses and accounting changes associated with its business combination and public listing, Securitize said.

Average tokenized assets rose 39% from a year ago to $5 billion, hitting an internal record. The firm's board of directors also authorized a $50 million share repurchase program.

Securitize's shares, which closed Monday at $6.97, surged 12.20% in after-hours trading to $7.82.

Meanwhile, Securitize announced on the same day that it has entered into a definitive agreement to acquire the credit infrastructure company, which is developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets.

The acquisition is expected to add a new layer of financing to Securitize, enabling the platform to offer asset-backed lending alongside its existing tokenized asset offerings. The transaction is expected to close in the fourth quarter.

"By combining Securitize's tokenization infrastructure with River One's warehouse financing technology, the companies aim to enable institutions to bring financial assets onchain and access financing against those assets through an integrated platform," Securitize wrote in a press release, further explaining that the transaction allows the firm to enter the $6 trillion global private asset-backed finance market.

Founded in 2025, River One was incubated by Framework Ventures and is backed by both Framework Ventures and Maple Finance. It specializes in warehouse financing backed by portfolios of income-generating assets, including loans, receivables, and other collateral.

River One is establishing ties with capital providers in the stablecoin ecosystem to finance high-quality, income-generating assets. In practice, it uses stablecoin capital to fund the facilities on the back end while borrowers draw and repay in ordinary dollars, so the crypto layer stays mostly invisible to them. This is to maintain traditional credit underwriting standards and legal protections while using blockchain to improve financing efficiency.

"River One brings a talented team, deep expertise in asset-backed financing and blockchain technology that will allow us to participate in asset origination and lending markets," said Carlos Domingo, Chairman and CEO of Securitize. "This opens up a significant new addressable market for Securitize, with opportunities for higher-value services, additional revenue streams and greater monetization across our platform."

Some minor spoilers for FBI can be found throughout this article.

Throughout FBI’s eight-season run, the CBS procedural has touched on a handful of topics that have hit serious and important notes. That’s continued as Season 9 hit the 2026 TV schedule. This week's episode dove into domestic violence, and star Juliana Aidén Martinez told me that while she was frustrated over the storyline, it ultimately led to something pretty awesome.

Martinez joined FBI during Season 8 as Special Agent Eva Ramos. While it is only the second season she’s been on the show, we've been slowly been learning more details about her personal life. Monday’s episode, “Ultimatum,” dug deeper into her family life when her mom called her for help to leave her abusive partner. Unfortunately, by the time Eva got to her mom’s house, she no longer wanted to leave.

This is something that’s been going on for four years now, and Eva wanted to be done with it all. For Martinez, she tells CinemaBlend that the story made her just as upset as Eva, maybe even more:

For me, I was really frustrated with the situation when I read it. Actively, my body wanted to get up. I cried. I was just like, ‘I don't understand. There has to be a way to fix this.’ Then I took a step back, and I was like, ‘That's probably such a relatable experience.’ When we think about it, statistically, every person knows someone that has been affected by domestic violence, whether they're aware of it or not.

FBI has had a lot of gruesome cases, but this kind of storyline can be particularly difficult to tackle. Having the kind of passion and frustration that Martinez had definitely helped fuel the episode, even if it was tough to get through.

For the actress, it came with an unexpected benefit: she learned about what New York is doing to help victims. Not only that, but Martinez was able to work alongside The Crime Victims Treatment Center to be able to tell the story authentically.

Through this episode, I've connected with the Crime Victims Treatment Center, which is New York State's first program created to support victims of sexual assault and violence. And it was actually really through connecting with them and learning what you do in this type of situation, which sometimes the most powerful thing you could do is give a person their autonomy and listen rather than to act on their interest.

Working alongside the CVTC gave Martinez a new perspective on the situation and how to better channel what Eva and her mom were going through. Even though it was a hard episode to film, being able to have that kind of knowledge and understanding will not only help Martinez in future episodes, but will maybe even impact Eva as a character.

That really informed me on Eva's interactions throughout the episode, and taught me what to do, right? And how to be a better supporter, better ally, better friend, daughter, etc. Because I was really upset and I was like, ‘Wow, what a relatable experience that you would want to do something about it.’

I don't know what will happen with the two in future episodes, Martinez is hopeful the resolution to this storyline will be satisfying. Fans have not seen much of Eva’s personal life and her family, and this small taste shows how much she cares for her mom. It’s why she initially told her she was done, because she didn’t want to see her in pain. If Martinez has her way, Eva and her mom will both be taking center stage again in the near future:

I would say the most important female relationship you have in your life as a daughter is your mom, and how much we love our mothers and vice versa. To me, the relationship between a mother and a daughter is so sacred, and it's also really powerful. And I hope, my fingers are crossed that we get to see that relationship evolve. We really see it evolve from the beginning of the episode to the end of the episode.

The popular crime procedural has delved into heavy topics before, and there’s no telling what will be next. The series doesn’t seem to shy away from emotional storylines no matter how hard they may be, but that’s what makes the series so great and so exciting. You never know what could be next. A major exit? Another storyline that ties in with real life?

FORT LAUDERDALE, Fla., Nov. 9, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Cloud Software Group Holdings, Inc. ("Cloud Software Group" or the "Company") today announced the expiration and final results of its previously announced cash tender offer (the "Offer") to purchase its 9.00% Senior Secured Second Lien Notes due 2029 (the "Notes") for the Consideration (as defined below), subject to a $2,000,000,000 cap on the aggregate principal amount of Notes purchased in the Offer (the "Tender Cap"), subject to proration and the terms and conditions set forth in the Offer to Purchase dated November 2, 2026 (as it may be amended or supplemented from time to time, the "Offer to Purchase"). Capitalized terms used but not defined in this press release have the meanings given to them in the Offer to Purchase.

The Offer expired at 5:00 p.m., New York City time, on November 9, 2026 (such time and date, the "Expiration Date"). Withdrawal rights for the Offer expired at the Expiration Date, and accordingly, Notes validly tendered in the Offer may no longer be withdrawn except where additional withdrawal rights are required by law.

At the Expiration Date, according to information provided by Global Bondholder Services Corporation, the tender and information agent for the Offer (the "Tender and Information Agent"), the aggregate principal amount of Notes validly tendered and not validly withdrawn pursuant to the Offer and the aggregate principal amount of Notes accepted for purchase, are set forth in the table below.

Aggregate Principal Amount Outstanding Prior to the Offer

9.00% Senior Secured Second Lien Notes due 2029

No representation is made as to the correctness or accuracy of the CUSIP numbers or ISINs listed herein or printed on the Notes. Such information is provided solely for the convenience of the Holders.

The consideration (the "Consideration") represents the amount payable per $1,000 principal amount of Notes validly tendered and accepted for purchase by the Company. The Consideration excludes accrued and unpaid interest ("Accrued Interest") on such Notes from and including the immediately preceding interest payment date for such Notes to, but excluding, November 16, 2026 (the "Settlement Date"), which will be paid in addition to the Consideration. Accrued Interest will cease to accrue on the Settlement Date for all Notes accepted in the Offer. Under no circumstances will any interest be payable because of any delay in the transmission of funds to Holders by The Depository Trust Company or its participants.

Notes validly tendered are subject to proration so that the Company accepts for purchase Notes in an aggregate principal amount up to the Tender Cap. The proration factor has been rounded to the nearest tenth of a percentage point for presentation purposes. The Company will multiply the principal amount of each valid tender of Notes by the proration factor and round the resulting amount down to the nearest $1,000 principal amount in order to determine the principal amount of such tender that will be accepted pursuant to the Offer. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

The company is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology. The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as its share price fell by more than 4%.

Trump later told reporters at the White House that the FBI was now looking into the issue, but did not elaborate on the probe or when it was launched. The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia, the company at the centre at the development of AI worldwide through its microchips. She is not related to the Nvidia chief.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

The American firm, co-founded by Huang, is also a major focus in the AI rivalry between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating the company's exports to Beijing as a matter of national security.

Huang and Nvidia were also sued on Monday in a proposed class action lawsuit in California accusing them of defrauding shareholders by concealing the chief executive's ties with a suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the firm lied to the media about Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked multiple times, including by the BBC, when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

Trump said he thought Huang was an "honorable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president said.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing, and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Nvidia designs advanced computer chips that power AI models and is at the centre of the US and China's technology race - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked national security fears.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Huang has admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

In the letter, the chairmen of the committees said they were seeking a briefing "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

It is unclear whether Huang will appear. Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday told the BBC that it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

What is Muse Ember? Meet Meta’s new low-cost AI model

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Booking a vacation sounds like one thing to hand off to an AI assistant, right up until it has to check the calendar, open a dozen tabs, and figure out whether the cheapest flight is still the cheapest once a carry-on is involved. Every one of those steps takes work, and when an AI agent is doing it, the cost can add up.

Meta has a new model built for that part of the job.

On Nov. 9, Meta's Superintelligence Labs introduced Muse Ember, which the company calls its smallest and most cost-efficient AI model yet. It's designed for the steps an agent might repeat dozens of times in a single assignment, like pulling details out of a document, calling another tool, or running a test after changing a line of code.

Muse is the app with the cute little avatar — named Jolly — that Meta has been promoting since September. The AI assistant can work across (some) apps with a user's permission. The app passed 5 million downloads in 22 days, and has already had many updates; last week, Meta started rolling out voice conversations and animated avatars for the assistant in the U.S. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Meta also uses the Muse name for a family of AI models. The company positions Muse Wonder as the model for its toughest problems and Muse Spark as a model for coding and other work that involves using tools. Ember is the cheaper option for repeatable tasks. Meta's pitch is that developers can use Wonder to plan a complicated job and hand off the individual steps to Ember. For now, Meta lists Ember as available through its Muse Code tool and developer API.

In early testing shared by Meta, cloud storage company Box said it used Ember to check contracts clause by clause against a company’s negotiation rules and assign an overall risk rating. Meta says the model can take in up to a million tokens at once, enough to work through large sets of documents, and can handle images, audio, and video alongside text. That gives developers room to build an agent that can read a scanned contract or pull details from a meeting recording.

The price is what makes Ember interesting. On Artificial Analysis's benchmark, Ember and Anthropic's Claude Haiku 5.5 both rounded to a score of 36 at their highest reasoning settings. Ember cost about 14 cents per benchmark task, compared with 41 cents for Haiku, even though the two have the same standard rates of 10 cents per million input tokens and 50 cents per million output tokens. OpenAI's GPT-6 Luna scored 28 and cost about 15 cents per task at its highest setting.

In Meta's published results, Ember beat Haiku on a terminal coding test, while Haiku did better on a test that judged whether code changes were ready to merge. Meta added that it already has even cheaper models in training.

As the company continues to bet that AI assistants will take on more everyday tasks, developers will need an affordable way to handle all the little steps behind the scenes. A flight search, for example, can mean sorting through dozens of fares, each with its own rules. If Meta wants people to hand off that kind of chore every day, it has to make checking the carry-on policy cheap on its end, too.

Creality Debuts mBox, A Kid-Operated AI 3D Printer, at $329

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

HOUSTON, Nov. 9, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Creality, a global leader in the 3D printing industry, today introduces mBox, an AI 3D printer designed specifically for kids. Featuring a 153 Ã 153 Ã 153 mm build volume, the kid-operated printer brings together kid-safe designs, simple printing operations, and accessible creative tools to help young makers turn their ideas into physical creations. Priced at $329, mBox is now available exclusively on TikTok Shop in the U.S. from November 9 to December 10.

mBox is built for families with children aged 6â16 who enjoy drawing, block building, model making, DIY projects or exploring technologies. It is particularly designed for young creators who want to go beyond just buying ready-made toys and instead turn their own imagination into tangible and personalized creations, whether through independent projects at home or hands-on activities in the company of parents. mBox is also ideal for STEAM learning spaces where educators seek to integrate safe and simple 3D printers into everyday classroom subjects, like science experiments, engineering design, and creative arts.

Developed specially for kids, mBox prioritizes safety in its product engineering. Its fully enclosed chamber keeps the nozzle and moving components behind an enclosure while children watch their creations take shape. During printing, the unheated build plate eliminates the need to handle a heated bed when removing finished prints, while activated carbon filtration helps filter impurities from the chamber air.

Notably, a configurable door-open alert offers three response options: no action, alert only, or alert and pause. When the alert-and-pause is set and triggered, printing stops and parents receive an app notification.

Together, these thoughtful design features help create a safe and friendly space for printing and creating, ensuring parents feel more at ease as children build 3D models on their own.

mBox focuses on simplified operations to make 3D printing easier for kids to get started with.

With the 3.97-inch full-color touchscreen, kids can pick a model and start printing in just a few clicks without connecting to a phone. Before printing, auto leveling simplifies print preparation and helps improve first-layer adhesion, reducing the need for parents' manual adjustments. During printing, the CoreXY motion system with vibration compensation supports stable printing and cleaner surface details, while AI detection uses the built-in camera to identify issues like warping, foreign objects, and spaghetti failures, and then alerts are sent to parents through the app.

In addition, there is RGB status lighting to show print progress and a low-noise mode which keeps operating noise at or under 46 dB.

Beyond the printer itself, mBox bridges kids with dedicated software, Creality Park App and Creality Park Studio. The Creality Park App is a 3D playground showcasing curated models and STEAM courses in a fun way and offering pick & print experience for younger users. For those ready to take their ideas further, the Creality Park Studio, a PC-based platform, offers approachable 3D modeling tools to help design original creations.

The ecosystem also incorporates AI tools CubeMe and SnapForm, enabling kids to turn photos into personalized, printable figurines, keychains, and accessories.

From a simple idea to a piece of creation, mBox aims to spark imagination and encourage hands-on making, helping young creators turn inspiration into something they can hold in hands.

mBox is now available exclusively on the U.S. TikTok Shop at the price of $329 from November 9 to December 10.

Meta introduces Muse Ember to power cost-efficient AI agents

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Meta Platforms Inc. today introduced Muse Ember, an entry-level artificial intelligence model that costs significantly less than the competition.

Notably, it’s the first AI that the company trained using Muse Wonder. The latter algorithm is the Facebook parent’s flagship large language model. Meta didn’t specify which part of the workflow it automated with Muse Wonder.

One possibility is that the company used it to perform post-training, the task of honing a model’s capabilities by training it on additional data. Thinking Machines Labs Inc. recently demonstrated that even a relatively small LLM can run post-training runs end-to-end. Anthropic PBC, for its part, last week revealed that its AI models could soon gain the ability to significantly accelerate machine learning research.

Applications often use frontier LLMs such as Muse Wonder as part of AI ensembles. When a user enters a request, the frontier LLM splits it into smaller tasks and assigns them to agents powered by a lower-cost model. Meta says Muse Ember is specifically optimized to play the latter role.

The company evaluated the model using the Artificial Analysis Index, which aggregates nine AI benchmarks spanning use cases such as coding and financial analysis. Meta says that Muse Ember matched the score of Anthropic’s entry-level Claude Haiku 5.5 model at about a third the price. It also outperformed OpenAI Group PBC’s comparable GPT-6 Luna algorithm.

The same internal evaluation saw Meta test Muse Ember and the two rival models across nine other benchmarks. All three algorithms were in “max” mode, which means they used the maximum amount of hardware at their disposal instead of prioritizing cost-efficiency. Muse Ember outperformed Haiku and Luna across seven of the benchmarks.

The area where the model appears to have the biggest edge is business task automation. Whereas most of Muse Ember’s benchmark results were only a few percentage points better than those of Haiku and Luna, it clocked a 17% lead on AutomationBench-AA. It’s a collection of 657 tasks that span popular cloud applications such as Gmail and Salesforce.

Muse Ember has a context window of 1 million tokens, which enables it to ingest lengthy business documents. Users can also include images, audio and video in their prompts. The model generates responses up to 512,000 tokens in length.

Muse Ember is available through Meta’s Model API for 10 cents per million input tokens and 50 cents per million output tokens. A built-in cache can reuse frequently used prompts to avoid duplicate processing. When the feature is active, Muse Ember’s pricing drops to 0.4 cents per million input tokens.

SoftBank Group shares surge 11% as Asian tech stocks track Wall Street rebound

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Asian technology stocks surged Tuesday, tracking a rebound on Wall Street overnight as a sharp decline in oil prices lifted sentiment while renewed buying in chip names spilled over into Asia, following last week's bruising sell-off.

Japan's technology investment giant SoftBank Group surged 12%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest gained 5.41%. Semiconductor equipment maker Tokyo Electron rose 5.88%, while Lasertec and Disco advanced 6.34% and 6.95%, respectively.

South Korean chipmaker SK Hynix jumped 8.01%, while Samsung Electronics rose 5.32%. Samsung SDI climbed 6.27%.

The gains followed a strong session on Wall Street, where the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite climbed 2.74%, while the S&P 500 rose 2.35% to clock its best day since April 8.

The recovery came after a steep sell-off in global technology stocks last week, fueled by concerns that Democrats' sweeping victory in the U.S. midterm elections could lead to tighter restrictions on data-center development, potentially impacting demand for artificial intelligence chips and infrastructure.

Beyond the political uncertainty surrounding potential restrictions on data-center development, investors have also grown increasingly wary of the enormous capital expenditures required to build out AI infrastructure. Questions over how quickly those investments will translate into meaningful profits have weighed on sentiment toward companies across the semiconductor supply chain.

Australia Releases AI Safety Recommendations After OpenAI Breach

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese released 18 recommendations on November 10, 2026, from a rapid review into an OpenAI agent’s unauthorized access to a Services Australia portal, the Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet said. The package covers AI-developer reporting, possible criminal offenses and government cyber defenses.

The recommendations are intended to prepare Australia for AI incidents of greater scale, complexity and consequence. Their status varies: some direct agencies to act, while others ask the government or named departments to consider, explore or review possible measures.

Recommendation 3 asks the Australian Government to consider mandatory AI safety reporting within set time limits, expressly including misaligned behavior and cyber incidents involving AI agents. Recommendation 4 separately asks officials to explore support for timely reporting of AI cyber or safety incidents and near misses, including possible protections for AI developers and others with relevant knowledge who disclose incidents or vulnerabilities. Together, they put reporting deadlines, near misses and disclosure protections on the policy agenda without imposing a new reporting duty now.

The Attorney-General’s Department, consulting the Australian Federal Police and Department of Home Affairs, is asked to consider criminal penalties for egregious regulatory non-compliance. Another option is a standalone failure-to-prevent offense covering cases where a company develops a model that engages in harmful conduct and fails to take reasonable preventive steps. The recommendation explores those possibilities; it does not itself create an offense.

Internationally, the Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade, the Australian Signals Directorate (ASD) and the Department of Industry, Science and Resources, including the Australian AI Safety Institute, are asked to continue work on consistent expectations for safety, incident reporting, evaluations, safeguards and transparency. Other recommendations call for engagement with OpenAI’s Australian Taskforce and similar initiatives, possible personnel exchanges and technical placements, and encouragement of frontier firms’ Australian research, training and safety activities. The government is also asked to consider the AI Safety Institute’s future capacity for technical research, evaluation and rapid advice during crisis coordination.

The Protective Security Board is asked to issue guidance clarifying agencies’ obligations and escalation expectations when a reported vulnerability or suspected cyber incident has not yet been technically validated. Commonwealth agencies are told to review vulnerability-disclosure and incident-management procedures so unusual incidents reach ASD, Home Affairs and senior decision-makers promptly. That focus addresses the notification chain described by ministers: OpenAI’s first notice went to Services Australia’s public vulnerability-disclosure inbox on September 10, 2026, and the agency alerted ASD on September 15, 2026, after checking the report.

Formal liaison arrangements are proposed among ASD’s Australian Cyber Security Centre, the National Office of Cyber Security and the AI Safety Institute to improve information sharing and identify incidents with broader national-interest implications. The National Office of Cyber Security would decide whether arrangements to manage an incident’s broader consequences are required. Recommendation 13 adds a dated step: agencies responsible for national crisis plans are asked to assess AI’s effects on their hazards and response arrangements and write to Crisis Arrangements Committee chairs with outcomes and proposed changes by the end of 2026.

Resilience measures call for higher staff AI literacy, renewed focus on fundamental cyber-security practices, including ASD’s Essentials, consolidation of government digital systems, and shutdown or segregation of systems that do not need internet connections. Agencies are also asked to work with ASD to identify and assess responsible uses of AI-enabled monitoring, penetration testing, threat detection and incident response. The portal itself is already inactive: Minister for Government Services Katy Gallagher said it would not be reactivated and its public data was being transferred to data.gov.au.

The review followed a June 18, 2026 incident in which OpenAI’s research team used an internal model for internet research into public medicine spending. After repeated blocks, the agent tried alternative routes and gained unauthorized access to public and non-public files. The standalone portal hosted aggregate Medicare and Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme statistics, not claims, payments or individual records; the ministers said no individual medical data was accessed. The government publicly disclosed the incident and announced the review on September 24, 2026.

The practical response now runs through government agencies, from escalation routes to legacy-system consolidation. The larger regulatory choice remains open: whether Australia will convert the reporting and criminal-law options into enforceable obligations for AI developers.

Creality Launches mBox for Children With AI Design and Print Monitoring

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Creality introduced the mBox on November 9, 2026, a $329 AI-equipped 3D printer designed for children aged 6–16. The enclosed machine combines a 153 × 153 × 153 mm build volume with camera-based print monitoring and tools that turn photographs or drawings into printable objects.

The announced U.S. sales window runs exclusively through TikTok Shop from November 9 through December 10, 2026. Creality is aiming at family projects and STEAM learning spaces, with uses including science experiments, engineering design and creative arts.

The mBox product page lists five protection layers: a fully enclosed chamber, door-open alert, cold-bed printing, activated carbon filtration and kid-friendly PLA. The enclosure separates children from the hot nozzle and moving components, while the unheated build plate means there is no heated bed to handle when removing finished prints.

The door-open response has three settings: no action, alert only, or alert and pause. Triggering the third option stops printing and sends parents a notification through the app, allowing the response to be matched to the level of supervision required.

Basic operation does not require a phone: the 3.97-inch full-color touchscreen can be used to choose a model and start printing. Automatic leveling reduces manual setup, while the motion system combines CoreXY with vibration compensation. Creality says camera-based AI detection identifies warping, foreign objects and spaghetti failures, then alerts parents through the app. RGB lighting reports print status.

AI is also used before fabrication begins. Creality says CubeMe and SnapForm can convert a family or pet photograph, or a child’s drawing, into a printable design without complex 3D modeling; figurines, keychains and accessories are among the example outputs. This moves the AI role upstream, helping create the object’s geometry as well as watching for problems while it is printed.

The surrounding software is divided by experience level. The Creality Park App supplies curated models, STEAM courses and a pick-and-print workflow for younger users. Creality Park Studio runs on PCs and provides simpler modeling tools for original designs, creating a progression from choosing ready-made models to designing new ones.

The fused-filament printer measures 290 × 340 × 335 mm and weighs 6.78 kg. Jobs can start from USB offline, the touchscreen, the app or Creality Park Studio, and connectivity includes 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi. Power-loss recovery and automatic filament loading are included.

Creality lists a typical speed of 200 mm/s with Hyper PLA and a maximum of 300 mm/s, alongside a low-noise mode rated at 46 dB or less. The 0.4 mm nozzle has a maximum temperature of 250°C, and layer thicknesses run from 0.1 to 0.35 mm. CFS and RFID filament are not supported; slicing software options are Creality Park Studio and Creality Print 7.0 or later.

The included 250 g spool of rainbow PLA changes colors as it prints, creating gradients without filament swaps. Paints and painting tools for decorating finished models are not included.

For homes and STEAM rooms, the mBox offers a guided form of independent fabrication: children can begin with a curated model, photograph or drawing, while AI fault detection, configurable door responses and app alerts place supervision inside the printing workflow.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/healthtap-unveils-virtual-primary-care-program-medicare-advantage-plans" hreflang="en">HealthTap unveils virtual primary care program for Medicare Advantage plans</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
HealthTap unveils virtual primary care program for Medicare Advantage plans" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

HealthTap is rolling out a new program for Medicare Advantage plans that seeks to help members form a connection with a primary care doctor that carries forward.

The program leans on a combination of proactive outreach and access to a patient's historical medical records to support plans in identifying needs or risks, filling care gaps and coordinating follow-up or other necessary care. It's built to directly support individuals who may not have a PCP on file.

HealthTap reaches out to those members proactively, the company said, and helps them schedule an initial virtual visit with a provider who stays connected for preventive services, chronic condition management and medication concerns.

"The angle that we're taking is: wouldn't it be cool if, in fact, your virtual primary care physician was just like a bricks and mortar primary care physician?" said Rick Cochran, senior vice president of health plans at HealthTap, in an interview with Fierce Healthcare.

In an ideal scenario, a patient who has a consistent primary care physician who they see over the course of multiple years and who they come to trust, he said. HealthTap is looking to build that experience with virtual primary care docs, and reach patients who are disconnected from the health system with critical services.

Cochran said that the team, at times, receives feedback about concern that a program like this could upset in-network practices. However, he said that HealthTap views these service lines as complementary, and the company as offering another way for insurers to reach members who are underserved and in need of help.

"There's a bunch of people who haven't been receiving care that we can work with," he said. "We're not attempting to disrupt anything. We're attempting to complement things."

Through the program, HealthTap can connect with members via text messaging, email, direct mail or phone, at the plan's discretion. The member can then schedule the appointment for themselves and choose a board-certified primary care physician based in their state.

Between video visits, the member can also connect with their physician through secure messaging, HealthTap said.

In aiming to make the experience feel as much like traditional, brick-and-mortar clinics as possible, Cochran said that members are allowed to switch their physicians and find the one that best matches their individual health needs.

And to better support the physicians, HealthTap pulls from health information networks to pull key medical records, including relevant data from before an individual enrolled in their current plan. That set-up also means that the patient can carry their relationship with a HealthTap doc forward should they switch plans, Cochran said.

Cochran said that COVID-19 really kicked down the door to virtual care for seniors, and there's significant opportunity in MA for a program like HealthTap's, as these patients often have multiple comorbidities and may face barriers to seeing a doctor in person, such as the lack of transportation.

The company did an initial deployment with one MA plan and found that 35% of the initial outreach cohort scheduled an appointment, and 19% completed their first visit within six weeks. Satisfaction scores for both patients and physicians are high, Cochran said.

The approach gives doctors more time to spend working "to make people better, not crank through visits," he said.

"Our physician satisfaction score is very high because our physicians have the time to spend with the patient to ensure that the patient's needs are met," he said.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/ai-enabled-virtual-mental-health-startup-siggy-launches-stealth" hreflang="en">AI-enabled virtual mental health startup Siggy launches from stealth</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
AI-enabled virtual mental health startup Siggy launches from stealth" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Siggy, an AI-enabled virtual mental health provider, has launched out of stealth with $6 million in pre-seed funding.

Siggy aims to build a complete mental health system that delivers continuous, personalized care. It combines therapy, prescription management and AI for intake and between-visits support. Intake is free; patients only pay if they accept treatment. Siggy is backed by Village Global, alongside several angel investors.

Patients begin with a free, anonymous conversation with Siggy’s AI that doesn’t require an account or login. It remembers everything, so patients don’t have to repeat their history when they eventually see a provider. An AI-powered preliminary diagnosis is reviewed by clinicians, and a patient is presented with treatment options. After a call with a human provider, patients log into Siggy to add existing providers or relatives to the system and confirm their treatment goals.

If a patient accepts treatment, they see a human clinician monthly with the option of engaging with Siggy’s AI between visits, which draws on their full history. Siggy’s therapy AI agent does not answer medical questions, instead directing them to human providers. It can help patients work through their goals, remind them of specific exercises and be a general sounding board. There are also safety protocols in place to flag higher-acuity needs to human providers.

Siggy can connect to wearables and offers a one-minute daily check-in on sleep, mood, how the body feels and medication side effects. These data combined form the Siggy Score, a single number out of 100 that patients and their clinicians see every day. When Siggy sees a pattern building, it flags the change to the patient’s clinician with the context behind it. Weekly check-ins in the first two months of treatment let clinicians decide whether to hold a dose, increase it or change a medication.

“It can build a really detailed picture of you rather fast, and the idea is if there are behavior patterns that are recurrent…[Siggy can] begin to help people gain agency and recognize them before they happen,” Siggy co-founder and CEO Jonathan Sabbagh told Fierce Healthcare.

Sabbagh co-founded Siggy with Edvard Engesaeth, M.D., now COO, an early Journey Clinical investor who co-founded Nurx. Siggy’s chief medical officer is Daniel Montville, M.D., a Mayo Clinic-trained psychiatrist. Sabbagh previously worked for two decades in finance before finding out he was living with chronic burnout and undiagnosed PTSD from childhood trauma.

Sabbagh sought psychedelic-assisted therapy and went back to school to study psychology before dropping out to co-found Journey Clinical, a large psychedelic-assisted therapy network in the U.S. with over 3,000 practices. “I saw a real gap where people drop off treatments, they don’t have continuous care,” Sabbagh told Fierce Healthcare.

About 16% of American adults take antidepressants, and a fifth annually drop out before completing the recommended course of treatment. “Prescriptions aren’t the problem … it really is a fragmented system where you don’t have therapists ever speaking with your prescriber,” Sabbagh said.

Siggy can prescribe medications, except controlled substances. Clinicians will taper patients off medications when they are ready. Longer-term, Siggy hopes to be able to identify other conditions through analytics on speech, labs, genetic testing and biomarkers, such as diabetes or ALS. It also hopes to build out neural datasets to support clinical trials for technologies like brain implants.

Siggy is covered by major commercial payers in all 50 states, including Blue Cross Blue Shield, Aetna, UnitedHealthcare and Humana. It also takes Medicaid, Medicare, TRICARE and is free for veterans.

“Our goal is to provide continuous care for life. We don’t really see meds as a feature. It is a combination of building persistent AI identity, being able to track progress and to make essentially personalized decisions that very few could afford,” Sabbagh said.
