How much Netflix cost in 2007 vs. 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company is notorious for frequent price hikes.

Movie fans of a certain age may vividly remember the sad, somewhat rapid decline of the old video store. Whether your store of choice was the nearest Blockbuster or an independent shop, you probably smile at the nostalgia of heading to the video store on a Friday or Saturday night, anxiously hoping that a copy of the hottest new release was in stock. Or, better yet, just returned by another renter so you could swoop in and snag the last one.

Then streaming came along, and video stores around the world began fading into irrelevance, with locations closing as streaming services continued to grow in popularity. In 2006, Blockbuster — the undisputed king of video rental stores — made a last-ditch effort to stay current by offering Total Access, which allowed customers to exchange their mail-ordered DVD rentals in physical stores.

If you've only ever known Netflix as a streaming service on your smart TV, tablet or phone, you might find it interesting that, in 2007, Netflix could only be streamed on a Windows PC with Internet Explorer. And the video quality wasn't great. After all, high-definition (720p and 1080i) was just starting to take off as a mainstream viewing format in 2007. Netflix's streaming library of roughly 1,000 titles was pretty lean, as well. Its first original series was still five years away, and the first original Netflix movie didn't debut until 2015. But none of this stopped its prices from increasing — a true Netflix staple.

Over the next decade, Netflix added some new plans like Premium, Standard, Basic, Standard-definition-only, Basic with ads, Ad-Free and other iterations. And with those plans typically came annual price hikes, usually ranging from $1 to $3 more per month depending on the plan.

Today, Netflix offers three plans. Standard with ads costs $8.99 per month and includes ad-supported access to most (but not all) shows and movies in the Netflix catalog, with the ability to stream and download in 1080p HD on two supported devices at a time.

The Standard plan is $19.99 per month and includes everything in the Standard with ads package (minus the ads, of course), but with completely unlimited access to Netflix TV shows, movies and games. It also offers the option to add one extra member who doesn't live with you to the plan for another $7.99 per month to let them endure commercials, or $9.99 a month for the ad-free experience.

Finally, the Premium plan costs $26.99 monthly and offers unlimited access to the entire Netflix library, with video available in 4K Ultra HD + HDR with Netflix spatial audio, the ability to watch Netflix on four supported devices simultaneously and download on six supported devices, and the option to add up to two more members who don't reside with you for the same respective add-on prices per month as Standard.

Netflix has become notorious for its near-annual price hikes over the past 20 years. But if you really analyze what you get today compared to what you got in 2007, one could argue that today's plans give you better value for your money. Of course prices have increased since 2007. It's been 20 years, and our economy has been put through the wringer a number of times during that span.

Hearts unbeaten home record obliterated as swashbuckling Falkirk earn 31-year first

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rampant Falkirk obliterated Hearts’ 18-month unbeaten home record in the Scottish Premiership with a stunning 5-2 win at Tynecastle as they leapfrogged the Jambos into third place.

Wouter Vrancken’s beleaguered side, missing 11 players through injury amid a gruelling fixture list, were in disarray as the swashbuckling Bairns romped into a five-goal lead early in the second half through Finley Barbrook, Owen Eames, Liam Henderson and Jacob Murrell’s double.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Hearts hit back through substitute Sabri Guendouz and a Claudio Braga penalty, but Falkirk were well worthy of making it five top-flight wins in a row for the first time since 1995.

It was a first league defeat at Tynecastle for Hearts since April 2025. To exacerbate their grim predicament, the Jambos, who have won just two of their last 11 matches, lost yet another senior player as Malachi Fagan-Walcott limped off in the second half.

There were two changes to the Hearts side that started Thursday’s Conference League defeat to Thun as Stephen Kingsley and Guendouz were replaced by Yan Dhanda and Landry Kabore.

Falkirk boss John McGlynn, named manager of the month for October, made one enforced change from the side that defeated Celtic last Sunday as Jack McMillan took over from injured right-back Ryan Strain.

The Bairns seized the lead in the fourth minute. After Murrell’s close-range effort from Scott Tanser’s cross was brilliantly saved by Beau Reus, Barbrook reacted quickest to drill home a low, angled finish from eight yards out.

Hearts threatened an equaliser in the 10th minute, but Kabore’s effort on the turn from the edge of the six-yard box was superbly blocked by Scott Bain.

Falkirk doubled their lead in the 17th minute after the hosts were caught wide open on the counter-attack. Dhanda’s shot from distance was charged down, allowing the Bairns to break swiftly, with Brad Spencer weighting his pass perfectly for Eames, who fired past the exposed Reus.

Braga thought he had pulled one back for Hearts in the 20th minute, but it was ruled out for handball.

Falkirk took full advantage of this reprieve by adding a third in the 33rd minute as Murrell peeled off Fagan-Walcott at the back post to nod home Deniss Melniks’ cross from the right.

The visitors struck again in first-half stoppage-time as Henderson curled high past Reus after Tanser’s corner broke to him just inside the box.

As jeers rained down from an irate home support, Vrancken replaced midfielders Laurent Mendy and Dhanda with Guendouz and Matthew Gillies for the start of the second half.

But within two minutes of the restart Falkirk notched again as Murrell fired home after being set up by Ethan Ross.

Hearts got off the mark when Guendouz drilled in his first goal for the club from just inside the box in the 50th minute.

Braga then tucked away a penalty in the 74th minute after Guendouz was tripped by Lewis Neilson, but it was not enough to prevent a third straight defeat for the Jambos.

Elvis and Eminem rights holders are suing Suno

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Suno is facing two more lawsuits over AI music clones.

Suno is facing two more lawsuits over AI music clones.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Suno is facing yet another pair of lawsuits. A group of rights holders of Elvis Presley’s music and the publisher of some of Eminem‘s biggest records are suing the AI music company, claiming it illegally generates music and lyrics that mimic those artists. The lawyers in the Presley suit claim that Suno has violated Tennessee’s Ensuring Likeness Voice and Image Security (ELVIS) Act, which makes it illegal for an AI company to clone a singer’s voice.

In the suit, lawyers representing Presley’s rights holders argue that “Suno has mechanically encoded itself with artists’ identities, hard-work, creativity, and time, and harnessed them all for a consistent conveyor belt ofcold hard cash.“ These suits closely mirror one filed by Jason Isbell and others in September, which claimed Suno created imitations of the plaintiffs but did not claim copyright infringement directly.

Later in the complaint, the lawyers write that “Suno’s ravenous race to the bottom aims to make human creativity obsolete, coldly replicating without a license in seconds what takes years to learn, struggle with, maintain, and endlessly attempt to make better and perfect.”

Suno spokesperson Cat McCormack told The Verge that, “Suno exists to help people create new, original music, not to trade on anyone’s name or likeness, and we prohibit any unauthorized use of an artist’s work. To protect against this, we do not allow prompts for specific artists’ names and songs and work with third-party providers to screen uploaded audio files and lyrics for potential misuse. We are always working to strengthen our guardrails and we will continue to add new safeguards that protect artists and our users.”

This adds to the pile of lawsuits Suno is currently facing from Sony, UMG, Isbell, Reservoir Media, and more.

French carmaker Renault wants to get into the military robot business

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Forget what your Fox News-addicted uncle told you about cheese-eating surrender monkeys. The French military has centuries of storied military victories and traditions behind it, and Renault wants in on that action with a new, autonomous electric vehicle platform designed to tackle the Armée de Terre’s most demanding missions.

Co-developed by the EV experts at Renault Group and the defense experts at Belgian electronics firm John Cockerill, the new Forefront concept previews a new, modular ground vehicle that can be rapidly industrialized at a controlled cost, with unique mobility and off-road performance, and capable of autonomously navigating complex terrain. The result is a machine that could front-line logistical capabilities for both defense and civilian applications, enabling missions to be carried out remotely in very high-risk and hard‑to‑reach environments.

“Forefront Concept … demonstrates the contribution that Renault Group’s industrial and technological expertise can make to a strategic sector such as unmanned ground vehicles with multiple applications expected across both civilian and military sectors in the years to come,” explains François Provost, CEO, Renault Group. “This innovative project brings together two complementary areas of expertise: Renault Group’s in the rapid, large-scale and cost-effective development and production of high-performance, reliable technological products and John Cockerill’s in the defense industry.”

Renault is quick to point out that its new concept isn’t just suitable for military work. Although it’s worth pointing out that the Renault-provided image, above, isn’t just AI slop: it would be impractical if it was real, with no real obvious way to get those RCP pipes on or off the thing. That said, presenting their military killbot as a construction mule is at least more credible than the “ambulance” and “troop transport” use cases we’ve seen touted by similar concepts on the American side.

Forefront seems to be little more than a rendering at this stage, with the first of the Forefront concept prototypes set to take part in a number of exercises and trials in the months ahead to further refine the concept.

Former Rangers star lands first managerial job as SPFL club appoint Europa League finalist

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

East Fife have appointed former Rangers midfielder Scott Arfield as their new player-manager.

The 38-year-old replaces Dick Campbell who was sacked by the League One club last month after a run of eight matches without a victory.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Arfield has been without a club since departing Livingston in May after taking on the role of caretaker player-manager for the final three matches of the 2025-26 season.

The move to Bayview represents a first full-time step into management for the former Canada international, who also had two spells with Falkirk as well as stints with Huddersfield Town, Burnley, Charlotte FC and Bolton Wanderers.

“I am absolutely delighted to be the new manager of East Fife and I cannot wait to get started,” Arfield, who has signed a contract until May 2029, told the club website.

“I feel I am ready to go into coaching after learning a lot from excellent managers during my playing career, and I am grateful to East Fife for believing in me and for giving me this opportunity.

“My aim is now to create opportunities for others and to build a team which will excite East Fife supporters and attract the attention of the local community. It is a highly respected club which has great potential, and it is a privilege to be given the chance to lead it forward.”

Liam Anderson, chairman of East Fife, added: “We are delighted to have agreed a deal with Scott to become our new player-manager.

“This an innovative appointment and gives a clear indication of our ambitions going forward. I am sure that the arrival of Scott will be welcomed by our fans who are keen to see an exciting brand of football at Bayview.”

Arfield, who played for Rangers in the Europa League final four years ago, will take charge of the Fifers for the first time when Peterhead visit Bayview on Saturday. He also hopes to resume playing this month once he regains match fitness.

An East Fife statement added: "The board of directors is grateful to the interim management team of Jim Moffat, Pat Slattery and Brian Easton, who have guided the squad through the recent period of vacancy. Jim will continue as goalkeeping coach, Pat will assist with coaching while injury prevents him from playing, and Brian returns to on-field duties."

Genoa, De Rossi: "Dispiaciuto per la sconfitta ma soddisfatto. Modric? Per chi ama il calcio è un piacere"

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nel post partita di Genoa-Milan Daniele De Rossi ha parlato ai microfoni di Sky Sport per commentare la sconfitta contro la formazione di Ruben Amorim. Di seguito le sue dichiarazioni.

Vedere un calciatore di 41 anni con questa passione è piacevole. Comandare la squadra la squadra insieme a Rabiot, peccato non averceli in squadra”.

Gloating Emails To A Newspaper Solved The Arson Spree That Hit 125 SUVs In One Night

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the early morning hours of August 22, 2003, in West Covina, California, vandals damaged one building and 125 SUVs across four dealerships and a private neighborhood with paint nd destroyed others with firebombs. The vandals spray-painted messages like "I love POLLUTION" and "SUV = TERRORISM". Most of all, they repeatedly painted the acronym "ELF," linking them to the Earth Liberation Front, a leaderless, loosely organized activist group. ELF targeted property and managed to avoid causing serious physical harm to anyone in hundreds of credited attacks from the late 1990s through the 2000s. The government branded ELF a terrorist group, partly because ELF used destructive tactics like arson, which is a common tool of militant groups. And, in fact, multiple SUVs were set on fire that night in 2003.

Either way, the government knew they were looking for passionate environmentalists. Within just a couple of weeks, the feds arrested a suspect, Josh Connole. Connole was a peace activist, vegetarian, and solar panel sales rep. He drove an EV and lived at a commune. All of those things seemed to make him a perfect fit for a particular kind of profile.

But Connole wasn't the guy. After his arrest was announced, a reporter at the LA Times received the first of multiple emails from "Tony Marsden" that read, "The police have the wrong man here. I was amongst those responsible for the SUV attacks."

Was the sender concerned about an innocent man paying for their crimes? Maybe, but some gloating was involved, too. "We left plenty of clues, none of them pointing to Josh," the message said, then added smugly, "The FBI hasn't seemed to pick up on any of them, which makes the whole ordeal very boring for us, the true culprits." To prove his involvement, he noted that he had spray-painted an equation by Leonhard Euler, an 18th-century Swiss mathematician, on one of the SUVs. That was a detail that hadn't been released to the public.

His boredom wouldn't last long, as the emails would be traced to his college, Caltech. Apparently, he couldn't be bothered to send the emails from another location. Law enforcement seized multiple computers from the school and found a cache of the email messages on one of the machines, sent right after the sender checked his own messages. The actual sender of the email, as you might have guessed, was not named "Tony Marsden." His name was William "Billy" Cottrell, and he was going for his PhD in theoretical physics at Caltech.

If Billy thought that he could successfully deny sending the emails or his involvement in the spree, that wouldn't last long. Investigators soon matched his DNA to a headband found at the scene of the crime. For Billy, the jig was up.

At first, Billy told investigators he wouldn't implicate anyone else in the spree, but later blamed the arson part of it entirely on his friends, Tyler Johnson and Michie Oe. According to Billy, whose statements are a little self-serving and should probably be taken with a grain of salt, Tyler and Michie showed up that night and convinced him to go spray-paint some SUVs with them. Billy wouldn't have seen this as an odd request, since he and Tyler had recently planned to plaster a couple of hundred SUVs with bumper stickers that said, "My SUV supports terrorism," though that plan fell through.

The three hit one dealership, then another before moving on to a private neighborhood where they vandalized some more SUVs. It was in that neighborhood where Tyler, according to Billy, set the first SUV on fire. Billy claimed that Tyler had never told him about plans to set anything on fire, and he objected when Tyler used the first firebomb. Supposedly, Tyler said it wouldn't happen again. But Tyler didn't stop, and more SUVs were set ablaze at the last dealership hit that night. In fact, a Molotov cocktail accidentally hit Billy's clothes, setting them on fire. He quickly took off his shirt, which is how his headband wound up on the ground for investigators to find.

A few emails later, police had Billy in handcuffs. Johnson and Oe were later named as unindicted co-conspirators, but had fled the country before they could be arrested.

Billy's defense team had few options. They had to draw a line between spray painting, which wasn't a federal crime, and arson, which, in this context, it was. But Billy's attorney came up with an additional defense — Asperger syndrome. Asperger syndrome is a diagnostic term, since dropped by the World Health Organization, for a form of Autism that does not include intellectual or language impairment. His attorney hired a medical professional who later diagnosed Billy with the condition.

The defense claimed that his diagnosed condition kept Billy from picking up on social cues that would have told him that Tyler was lying about stopping at just one firebombing. When it came to figuring out other people's motives, as reported by Los Angeles Magazine, his lawyer asserted that Billy's brain was like "'a slow computer' with a dial-up Internet connection." That seems like an odd way to describe the brain of a doctoral candidate researching theoretical physics. Still, Billy did fit the common, if ill-informed, stereotypes of adults with Autism spectrum disorder with high intelligence. Why didn't he bother to drive to another location to send those emails? He had to get back to his theoretical physics work.

It could be considered a questionable defense strategy, given the estimated 5 million U.S. adults with Autism Spectrum Disorder and most of them, we assume, don't go around vandalizing SUVs. The judge would rule that Asperger's couldn't be used as a defense, but that ruling didn't come until the prosecution had almost rested its case.

A jury found Billy guilty of seven counts of arson and conspiracy to commit arson. The judge sentenced him to over eight years, which included three years added because of the supposed terrorism aspect of the crime. He was also ordered to pay $3.5 million in restitution.

Billy didn't do well in prison, even getting into a fight. It seems he tried to change the channel on the TV to the news when the other inmates wanted to watch "I Love Lucy". His ordeal prompted one of his former professors to write a letter detailing the emotional effects prison conditions were having on Billy. The professor got several prominent physicists, including Stephen Hawking, to sign the letter. It turns out that Billy's case had prompted an intense interest in the scientific community, with many expressing the view that imprisoning someone of his intelligence was depriving the world of the theoretical advancements he could be making. The letter worked, and Billy would be transferred to a different prison to serve out the rest of his sentence.

Eventually, the arson counts were thrown out, and Billy's entire sentence was vacated on appeal because his Asperger's diagnosis wasn't allowed to be used as a defense. The appellate court kept his conspiracy conviction, though, and sent the case back to the trial judge, who resentenced Billy to eight years for the remaining charge, meaning he would have to serve the same amount of time.

Billy was finally released in 2011. After he got out, he finished his PhD in 2017 at the University of Wisconsin–Madison, then held postdoctoral positions at the University of Amsterdam and Stanford University. He would go on to work at Jump Trading and teach a course on cryptocurrencies at the University of Chicago. Overall, things didn't turn out too bad for him. After all, the government has vowed to seek harsh penalties for this kind of crime, and the current administration has even threatened to send perpetrators of similar crimes to El Salvador.

What about his friends? Tyler Johnson fled to Europe and eventually wound up on the island of Corsica. He got by working odd jobs and even founded a nonprofit that supplied solar-powered ovens to households in impoverished communities. He also liked to hike the mountains, which would lead to his tragic death in 2010 in an avalanche. A documentary entitled "Finding Tyler" would be filmed about him after his death.

As far as we can tell, Michie Oe has never been caught. Her name doesn't appear in any law enforcement arrest databases we can search. Whether Billy actually participated in the arson that night or was only a willing participant in the spray painting is anyone's guess. But so far, he's the only one who has paid the price for what happened.

TechCrunch Mobility: The Cybertruck experiment is over

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and now, more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility!

Almost seven years ago, while clutching a laptop, my phone, and a notebook in a sea of Tesla fans, I watched head designer Franz von Holzhausen throw a metal, baseball-sized ball at the driver’s-side window of the Cybertruck. The result was cracked glass — actually, two by the end of the presentation — and some awkward jokes and chuckling by CEO Elon Musk.

I have witnessed a number of stunning, odd, and fantastical moments at Tesla events. But the Cybertruck reveal was the most interesting — and not because of the postapocalyptic design. It was the crowd.

When the truck rolled out onto the stage, I watched attendees gasp, some in revulsion. There was mumbling (“This certainly can’t be the Cybertruck?” I heard Tesla superfans whisper). And yes, there were cheers. The momentary shock of Musk’s biggest fans was quickly replaced by support, acceptance, and finally, endorsement.

It moved like a wave over the crowd. By the time attendees were waiting in line for Cybertruck rides, the mood had shifted. Everyone was a fan.

That fandom carried over to Cybertruck reservations, which skyrocketed to 1 million, Musk said during a third-quarter earnings call in 2023. But it never made the leap to actual sales. Industry experts estimate about 71,000 Cybertrucks have been sold since the first deliveries began in November 2023.

And now the Cybertruck is headed to the scrap pile. Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck next year, Musk said Friday.

Was the vehicle too edgy? Maybe, but this was always going to be a niche market. I believe more practical reasons dampened sales. Musk promised the Cybertruck would have a 500-mile range and a price tag under $40,000. It achieved neither, senior reporter Sean O’Kane noted in his report.

Zipline has had quite the week. Strike that — quite the year. The autonomous drone delivery startup, which was founded in 2014, has steadily scaled over the past few years. But 2026 has taken the company to new heights.

In early 2026, Zipline raised $600 million and quickly added another $200 million to that Series H round. The company planned to use these funds to accelerate its expansion to at least four U.S. cities in 2026.

Now it has even more money to grow even faster. Zipline announced this week it raised $1.5 billion and ramped up its partnership with Walmart to add 300 drone delivery sites across the United States. Zipline is now valued at $21.5 billion.

Electric truck startup Harbinger raised a $240 million Series D funding round as it expands its portfolio of products, including battery storage and defense applications. No word on the company’s valuation.

Humble Robotics raised $51 million in a Series A round, bringing total funding to $75 million less than a year after founding. The round was led by Obvious Ventures with participation from insiders Eclipse, Energy Impact Partners, and others.

Static Media acquired automotive news site CarScoops, adding to its existing portfolio that includes 34 brands such as Engadget, Jalopnik, and SlashGear.

Also, the micromobility startup spun out of Rivian in 2025, quietly started and then walked away from an electric two-wheeler pilot project in Kenya earlier this year, as it sought to focus its resources on its consumer e-bike and autonomous vehicle ambitions.

Amazon has made Uber Eats its official restaurant delivery partner for Prime members in the United States, effectively ending the e-commerce giant’s existing deal with Grubhub.

The Luce, Ferrari’s first electric car and one of its most polarizing models, is “fully allocated” for 2027.

Ford unveiled what it’s calling a “privacy promise” — an “explicit commitment” to keep customers’ vehicle data safe and give them control over what they share. Ford also launched a website designed to educate customers about how, where, and why the automaker uses their data, and to show them how to change those settings.

May Mobility and Forterra advanced to phase three of the Department of Defense’s Robotic Operations for Autonomous Delivery and Sustainment (ROADS) prize challenge, an initiative to test and adopt commercial self-driving vehicles in the military.

Serve Robotics announced plans to launch a new business line called Physical AI Services. The company will still operate and expand its autonomous delivery operations and become a technology supplier to other companies building robots.

The predictions of AI agents booking restaurants, trips, and even rides for the masses hasn’t quite materialized yet. Uber reported during its third-quarter earnings call that only four rides were ordered through an AI agent during its busiest week in October: the week of Halloween.

Waymo is shaking up its Uber relationship, confirming months of speculation. The company will end its exclusivity with Uber in the Austin and Atlanta markets starting in January 2028.

Over the past three years, this newsletter often highlighted an investigation, scoop, or interview from senior reporter Sean O’Kane. Some of my favorites were the articles we collaborated on, including his scoop that Jeff Bezos was backing Slate Auto, our story about how Jony Ive’s LoveFrom had worked on the design of the Also e-bike, his investigation into Fisker, and his coverage of how Waymo was relying on first responders to help move its robotaxis.

We have co-bylined dozens of articles on company earnings and breaking news and co-hosted Equity, TechCrunch’s flagship podcast about the business of startups. And so it’s bittersweet to see him head off on a new adventure at the Wall Street Journal to cover Tesla.

Congrats on the gig, Sean! We hate to see you go!

I will, of course, continue to deliver readers insider details, scoops, and analysis about the future of transportation, from autonomous vehicle technology and EVs to automotive tech and the future of flight.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Thankfully Resists FromSoftware’s Influence

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ocarina of Time sticks to the basics, and the remake is better for it

This year in video games, I’ve swung a digital sword more times than I can count. Thrilling action games like Onimusha: Way of the Sword and Phantom Blade Zero have gotten the feel of a sword fight down to a science, as steel clashes and sparks fly in spectacular fashion. But my favorite duels of the year aren’t in any of this year’s most razor-sharp action games; they’re all in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time.

The Switch 2 remake builds on the combat of Link’s 1998 adventure in careful but effective fashion. The Master Sword feels more powerful than ever thanks to killer sound design and a bit of visual pizazz on impact. What the remake doesn’t do, however, is change Link’s moves to give him the most widely used trick in modern action games: the parry. There’s no deflection to be found in Ocarina of Time’s remake, and that makes a seemingly simple combat system stand out in an unexpectedly refreshing way.

Compared to many modern games, Ocarina of Time’s swordplay doesn’t require much mastery from players. Aside from a charged spin attack and a leaping slash, Link’s offensive options are limited. Lock on, swing the sword, hit the enemy, and that’s basically all you need to know. Sure, you can block attacks with your shield or use one of your items to switch it up, but you’re always going to follow the same motions to take out a Deku Baba with consistent strikes.

One of my questions heading into the remake was about how much Nintendo would modernize that approach. After all, lots of remakes and remasters these days make reworked combat a priority; just this year, we’ve seen that in projects like The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt Remastered and Assassin’s Creed Black Flag Resynced. Rather than following that wave, Nintendo chose to keep Link’s offense relatively unchanged. That can take some getting used to if you play a lot of modern action games that all seem to have some version of a parry, or at least some kind of counterattack.

The quicker you acclimate to Ocarina of Time’s throwback action, though, the quicker you’ll come to appreciate its elegance. Fights aren’t meant to be long duels against evenly-matched opponents; Link is a master of his craft and can dispatch virtually any enemy in a few hits. Precision and timing are key as you patiently wait for your opening before lunging forward with a strike. The same goes for Link’s evasive maneuvers, which allow him to sidestep and backflip away from incoming attacks. It only takes a few Stalfos encounters to learn their attacks and lock down the right dodge and attack follow-up timing to take one out quickly.

In my review, I compared that to the experience of playing Punch-Out!!. The classic NES game’s staying power comes from the fact that every fight hinges on you learning each enemy’s tells and reacting to them accordingly. Every boxing match is a dance, and you have to learn your half of it. Ocarina of Time functions the same way, pushing you to hit all your marks rather than rush through a careful fight.

There are several fights in Ocarina of Time that test your ability to follow that flow, especially the late-game Iron Knuckles who hit like a truck if you fail to dodge their attacks. The best skill check, though, comes during a clash with Dark Link in the Water Temple. It’s the one time where you find yourself up against a fighter who is as efficient and precise as you, because it is you. Patience is your greatest skill in a battle that will taunt you for swinging wildly while your shadowy foe seemingly has an answer to every mistake you make. It plays out like a theatrically staged fencing match. Once you feel that, every other fight clicks into place, too.

Though it may sound a little simple, Nintendo finds the right way to make every slash feel powerful. Every sword hit is accompanied by an impactful thwack and an explosion of colorful particles. Even slashing the weakest enemies as Young Link feels spectacular, as if your blade has busted them open like a pinata. Those small touches make every hit feel important and satisfying to execute, rather than futile hits against a sponge. I can’t say the same for something like GameFreak’s summer not-quite-a-Soulslike Beast of Reincarnation, where bosses fall to death by a thousand cuts.

It may sound strange to call a combat system rooted in 1998 design fresh, but it really is in a world where so many action games I play feel like variations on a theme. Ocarina of Time may not impress modern players who look for expression in their combat, but you won’t hear anyone labeling it a Soulslike or dubbing it “parryslop.” The battles have an unmistakable texture to them, born out of a focused design philosophy that suits an equally focused swordsman. Who needs a parry when no blade can cross you?

Nintendo’s excellent Switch 2 remake recaptures the soul of a masterpiece

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

I’m also very impressed Nintendo was able to resist the temptation to emulate Balatro.

Cosa serve al Milan per essere competitivo? Amorim sicuro: "La mentalità"

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ruben Amorim nel suo intervento ai microfoni di DAZN al termine di Genoa-Milan 1-2 ha parlato di quello che serve alla sua squadra per essere davvero competitiva e soprattutto poterlo fare con continuità, anche considerando il doppio impegno tra campionato e competizioni europee. L'estratto con la risposta del tecnico rossonero.

“È la mentalità. Quando sei un giocatore non ci sono “giorni liberi”. Se giochi per il Milan, per questi club, non ci sono giorni liberi. Non puoi presentarti come è successo giovedì e poi riaccenderti per la domenica. Non è possibile, non è una cosa che funzionerà. Non è una condizione fisica, è una questione di avere ansia per la sconfitta. Se hai “panico” per la sconfitta non è che hai paura di perdere, ma sei consapevole che perdere è la fine del mondo. Questo è il feeling che voglio che abbiano le mie squadre”.

Barnsley FC and Daniel Stendel welcomed fun back like an old friend in FA Cup win v Aldershot Town - analysis

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As Daniel Stendel sat down to dissect Barnsley’s 7-2 FA Cup win over Aldershot Town, he offered a wry smile.

He had been asked about the two goals his side leaked late in a game that had already been taken well beyond the opposition.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

It was a question he will have known was coming, and a question most managers would have enjoyed answering to demonstrate their forensic and demanding nature. The word ‘standards’ would have most likely been used.

Stendel, however, had no interest in poking holes in a mostly scintillating performance. And who could blame him?

Barnsley’s campaign has been largely devoid of fun and rife with simmering discontent. With Bonfire Night celebrations spilling over into the weekend, Stendel clearly felt the Reds needed to simply enjoy the sparks.

“We keep the positive things,” said Stendel. “It's normal that I think about the two goals, but I think we analysed a lot in the last couple of weeks about some situations, some games, and why we didn't win the game in the end.

“I think today is time to say ‘good result, we're in the hat for the next draw’.”

Barnsley flew out of the traps at a dizzying pace and the brakes were simply never applied. By half-time, Cameron McGeehan and Vimal Yoganathan braces and a Reyes Cleary effort had Aldershot dead and buried.

McGeehan completed his hat-trick after the break and Rylan Brownlie got in the act, with the latter rounding off the afternoon after Aldershot had twice hit back via Annisuis Lewis.

“It was good to see that we used the spaces that we get, and a goal after a set-piece.

“I think three goals after crosses was good, especially that. It's one thing that we try to change and what we train in training, why we said we have to cross more balls in the box. And yeah, it was good today.”

Toxicity has enveloped Oakwell in recent months and while it has not been Stendel’s doing, it is clear he is relishing the trickling return of some positivity.

His side’s hard-fought draw with Oxford United a week prior evoked nostalgia, memories of a first spell in which supporter connection was strong.

They did not require the same spirit against the Shots but bluntly, it was just nice to see some goalscoring fun. It is a pleasure with which the Oakwell faithful have not been well-acquainted of late.

“We needed this game,” said Stendel. “It's a good opportunity for everybody to prove what we want to do next week and to get some confidence in defence and offence for winning duels, try to do things, cross the ball early, and in the box you can score goals.

“We have tough weekends in the past, and I think it was good for everybody, for the mindset, for the training time next week, for the game, especially before the weekend game.”

Barnsley will host Oldham Athletic in the Jersey Mike’s Trophy on Wednesday (November 11) but it is clear Stendel’s primary focus is on the Brick Community Stadium. That is where Wigan Athletic will entertain the Reds in a battle of two of League One’s bottom four sides on Saturday (November 14).

A battling league draw followed by a thorough dismantling of an inferior FA Cup opponent represents a start - but it would take a league win for the phrase ‘turning point’ to be uttered with any conviction. At present, it can only be whispered.

“I think we play sometimes really well, but we get no points for that,” said Stendel. “You have to score, you have to defend.

“I think it's important that everybody looks for situations that you can score, not only our strikers.

“I think this is important, especially for the next weeks when we have some games against teams around our situation in the table, but also we talk a lot about our defence behaviour, we have to improve that, and I think it was a good step in the right direction today.”

Barnsley: Slicker, Okedina, Gale (Noslin 76), Kay, Larkeche; Johnson (Kelly 63), Connell; Yoganathan, McGeehan (Taylor 71), Cleary (Brownlie 63); Adeyemo (Kirk 45).

Unused substitutes: Ravenhill, Smith, Richards, Alker.

Aldershot Town: Hayes, Dyce (Robinson 60), Oshilaja, Stickland, Harfield (Adom-Malaki 60); Borgnis, Odusanya; Peart (Gilbert 72), Tima, Sandah (Lewis-Evans 78); Abrahams (Lewis 60).

Unused substitutes: Patmore, Matton, Wyllie, Dinanga.
